ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e199	This is not the most lucid of all that the Storting is discussing, but the more important it is. So I just want to, on behalf of the committee, say some words. To take the first step: the national accounts are the annual accounts of the country's income and expenditure in the previous year. The accounts consist of two parts, appropriations and capital accounts. The accounts of appropriations include the items allocated to the State budget. It provides the basis for monitoring that the decision to grant the Trustee in this room is followed up by the central government. The capital account provides an overview of the country's assets, liabilities and cash stocks. There is little doubt that the financial statements for 2010 are comprehensive, as they are every year. The material is definitely not readily available and can be perceived as quite detailed and technical. Nevertheless, fortunately, it is a unanimous committee which supports the suspension of the notification. So I'm just going to make a few short additional comments. The oil-corrected government deficit for 2010 was 104.1 billion. kr. That's $5.3 billion. kr lower than in the newly aged budget, adopted in December 2010. The tax revenue was about 4 billion. kr higher than in the newly aged budget. Other increases in revenue included total revenue amounted to 7.4 billion. kr higher, while the cost was 2.1 billion. kr higher than in the newly aged budget. If we go on to the money we have on the book, we see that the book value of the state pension fund abroad increased by almost an incredible 439 billion. kr, from 2642 billion. NOK at the end of 2009 to nearly SEK 3,081 billion. kr at the end of 2010. The surplus increased the value of the fund by 252 billion. kr, while the net price gain increased the fund by more than 166 billion. kr. The capital injection in the liquidation of the State Petroleum Insurance Fund increased the capital of the Fund by nearly 20 billion. kr. These are quite inconceivable sizes that we handle through the State pension fund abroad, which will be dealt with in our own case just after we have completed the state accounts in the hall. But it's not just SPU that does it well. SPN — i.e. the domestic part of the pension fund — has also gained increased income. From the end of 2009 until the end of 2010, the capital of the State pension fund in Norway increased by nearly 18 billion. kr, from 117 b.w. to 135 B.C.E. kr. The change comes from net interest income and dividends of SEK 4.4 billion. kr and a total gain on share and interest rate positions of SEK 13,4 billion. kr. That was the numbers. There are large sizes we handle, and the numbers show us first and foremost how wonderfully privileged we are to be citizens of a country that has such government finances. Together with our combined work, it is the vast wealth of oil and gas that has enabled us to be in a completely different position, and to have a completely different development in wealth than many other countries. When, in addition, the aftermath of the financial crisis and the consequences of record debt in many countries continue to hit these countries very hard, it is only to note that we are once again in a situation we should be happy for at home, and we should be grateful for that. Thanks to both the oil money and the fact that we are very good at standing on and working – many are on and doing a fantastic job every day, and many are working – we get significant income, as the accounts here show, the community benefits. But this wealth also requires that we take responsibility, that we manage the values well, so that future generations too can enjoy them. Such a fortune does not come – and does not remain great – but it does matter how we deal, among other things. Here at the Stortinget. It is therefore gratifying that there is a broad majority in the Storting that wants to set aside a significant surplus in the accounts and transfer it to the State pension fund abroad every year, also for the year we are now dealing with. We must ensure that the extra resources we receive, including: from the oil, is not phased into the economy at such a rate that the economy is over. Our now large fund is the result of political elections over quite a long time. But finally, back to the numbers. I mentioned that the tax and tax revenues were about 4 billion. kr higher than in the newly aged budget. This suggests that things are going better – yes, actually good – in Norway. People are at work, and companies run well. Due to the financial crisis, the tax revenue from enterprises was expected to fall, but also these revenues have increased in 2009. In fact, tax receipts from entities have been affected to an unexpected extent little by the financial crisis and the international setback. It is good for the income of the community and the state, and it is also a result of a number of policy choices over a long period of time. Prosperity and income do not come of their own. The figures from the national accounts show the results of a successful policy. With this, I'm going to present the setting.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e258	State pension funds abroad, in the public currency called the Oil Fund, passed 3 000 billion for the first time in October 2010. market value kr. At the turn of the year, the market value of the State pension fund abroad was 3 077 billion. kr, and the market value of the State pension fund Norway was SEK 133 billion. kr. Another thought-provoking milestone was reached for approx. about a week ago. 15 years after Minister of Finance Sigbjørn Johnsen made the first deposit. With approx. 3 100 B.C.E. kr for management, the oil fund is now considered for the first time to be the largest government fund in the world. It is assumed that the state fund of Abu Dhabi was then transferred to the United Arab Emirates. There is a cross-political consensus that the Fund should maximise its return within moderate and conservative risk tolerance. In the 2011 national budget, the amount of the Fund is estimated to be more than 6000 billion. kr at the beginning of 2020. The entire Financial Committee underlines the importance that if the Community's savings are to benefit both today's generation and future generations, we are dependent on long-term and safe management and broad integration and a high degree of legitimacy around the way funds of the State pension fund are managed. I noticed that the government of the Minister of Finance in his press release on this announcement, Meld. St. 15 for 2010–2011, The Management of Staten pension funds in 2010, wrote the following: «In contrast to traditional pension funds, our savings are not designated as specific liabilities such as current pension payments.» I therefore note that the fund has nothing to do with pensions, and was thus given confirmation that it has been and is a rhetorical grip designed to transfer less of the state's enormous profits to the people. We in the Progress Party therefore believe that the name of the fund is directly misleading and that someone should consider changing it. State pension funds — or whatever they call it — are designed to save to future generations so that they can also benefit from the petroleum industry. But it is also an obligation that we build the nation further for the nation's population, so that its present and future business life will be well-equipped and have good conditions for asserting itself and being competitive in international trade. In February 2011, the Central Bank Chief Øystein Olsen placed great emphasis on the implementation of the rule of action, which is also a designed rule. The central bank manager pointed out that the action room was used ‘to implement standard increases and increase expenditure in a number of areas. On the other hand, priority should be given to measures that strengthen productivity and growth in the longer term, including fiscal adjustments, which seem to have been taken into account.’» I therefore hope that the government parties will now also focus on increasing the use of oil money being channelled to, for example. research, infrastructure and growth-promoting tax relief, as intended and should be, that most people, taxpayers, retain more of their own money and that the fund is used to generate economic growth. Just as when you or I own a house, an apartment or a farm, for that matter, we are occupied with having it in the best possible condition, and maintain it. Maybe, hopefully, we'll make it even better than it was when we took it, so that our heirs can take over a property that can meet all sorts of future storms and weather types. A sensible owner does not let his house fall due, has all his money in the bank and spends the interest money for consumption and party – but invests. We have had a hearing in the Financial Committee in connection with this big business communication. What makes me a little concerned is the lack of interest. Only a handful of stakeholder organisations reported their interest and met. It wasn't people, institutions, organizations or companies who had opinions about how the fund could get better returns, or maybe less risk, or more transparency; they were mainly concerned with ethics and the environment, which is so far well and good, but in my opinion it should have been more than the rainforest fund, WWF Norway, the Forum for Development and the Environment, and the organisation Delete U-lands debt that met. There was no input from the financial industry or the research community itself, perhaps because we have too little research environment in Norway in this area. The only thing was a written recording from blogger Lars Haakon Søraas, for other alternates to the Norwegian Bank's representation, and Morten Josefsen, who is a blogger and doctoral student at Handelshöjskolan BI. We all note that there has been a new debate in the media. This week it was interesting to read the recordings from the investor and billionaire Øystein Stray Spetalen. He stretched out this week again to the oil fund and asked directly, "How long shall we sit and not make money on shares?" I quote: ‘We started in 1998 by investing in shares, and until now we have not earned it. We haven't made money on this in 13 years, in the most amazing economy we've ever had." I recall the warnings that the Progress Party came with when the majority of the population here wanted to increase their share from 40 to 60 p.m., which happened against the votes of the Progress Party. In addition, the financier Øystein Stray Spetalen, like the Progress Party, believes that Norway should have built up administrative competence itself, mainly here in Norway, while at the same time being aware of who is in charge of the administration. In the past, the Progress Party has called for and proposed that we establish a Norwegian capital management environment in the light of the investments made through the State pension fund, so that Norway and the population benefit more fully, just as we have built in time, and continue to build an environment around oil technology, on which Norway is now the world leader. I also recall that many years ago the Progress Party first called for the Fund to invest in property. The central government pension fund made its first unlisted property investment on 13 January 2011. The mandate now provides for the Bank of Norway to place up to 5 pst. by the State pension fund foreign capital held in its own property portfolio by reducing the bond portfolio accordingly. It is expected that building such a large portfolio will take several years, as confirmed by NBIM during the consultation, while, of course, increasing investment in the coming years is expected. The entire Financial Committee considers it important to use the time necessary to ensure the proper phasing-in of investment in the property market. The Financial Committee has noted that, in the view of the Ministry, a re-examination of relevant research indicates that the foreign currency risk of the State pension fund is less than previously assumed, and in any case relatively small, so that it no longer appears to be the basis for having such a strong concentration of investment in Europe as the Fund currently has. As you can see: ‘The world’s production capacity and securities markets are more and more concentrated in other parts of the world. Over time, therefore, the share of the fund invested in Europe should be reduced for the benefit of larger shares in the rest of the world.’» This takes note of the Financial Committee but at the same time wishes to underline the importance of the European market for investment abroad in the State pension fund and that the European market will continue to be very important. As stated in the Communication, the Ministry will continue to work on the establishment of a new geographical breakdown. The Financial Committee assumes that changes with significant implications for the risk and expected return of the Fund will be submitted to the Storting before they are implemented. I also recall that for several years it has been considered to set up a separate fund for investment in emerging and less developed markets, which is in line with the conclusions of the Development Committee and the recommendations of Nordfund. It is therefore rather disappointing that no proposal is being made this time to set up a separate investment fund in emerging and less developed markets. This has been exposed a number of times. The party of progress is united with the Christian People's Party and the Left to wish for progress in this process, and to set up a separate fund for this. It is interesting and thoughtful that the management costs framework has remained stable over several years despite strong growth in the size of the fund. The figures for 2010 show a sharp increase in administrative costs, and the 2011 budget shows an increase in fees to external administrators of 39 p.m. in relation to 2010. During the consultation in the Finance Committee of the Storting, the Minister of Finance and the head of NBIM said that due to the size of the fund, SPU has economies of scale and is attractive to external administrators and that NBIM has reached good agreements with external administrators. Then I think that the cost framework should also reflect this. I appreciate the fact that there has become more transparency around the administration and I want the salary and bonus conditions of the administrators, including benefits, to be clarified in Norway's Bank's future reports. There was no lack of criticism and warnings after the fund's administrators received large bonus payments in the 2008 crisis year, a year when the fund had a negative return of 633 billion. kr. Nevertheless, we could record that extremely large payments to administrators also occurred in 2010, including a payout of 500 million. kr to an administrator. The Progress Party considers that there is no evidence that active management provides the best return in the long term. We are concerned about the development of costs in the fund and want, among other things, to: therefore an index-based management strategy for the Fund. The management of State pension funds should be done more according to global index references. Such a strategy, in the opinion of both our and some financial researchers, will reduce both the risk and the costs of management. At the same time, an index-based administration will enable Norway to establish a world-leading management environment in this form of management. In order to test and compare results more closely for different management strategies, the Progress Party proposes that the Fund be split into two, where 5 pst. the fund is managed within the current regime, while the remaining 95 pst. by the fund is transferred to passive index-based management. In order to achieve competition, we also want the passive part to be divided into two parts, one of which is managed under NBIM and the other part is put to tender. Finally, I would like to make a few comments – for our part pleasant comments – that there are political changes in the way of views on the management of the oil fund. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e348	This year’s Communication on State pension funds deals with the 2010 performance of the Fund and assessments of the framework and investment strategy of the Fund. As in 2009, the result for 2010 is very good. The return on the state pension fund abroad — SPU — was almost 10 p.m. and in the State pension fund Norway — SPN — at about 15 p.m. This means that we can soon stop talking about the large value losses during the financial crisis, because these are more than offset by the gains in the rise period that followed. The objective of the active management is to provide the fund with a higher return than the reference portfolio, thus creating an added value from which future generations can benefit. The results for 2010 are also good both in SPU and SPN. Nevertheless, there is still considerable uncertainty about the further development of financial markets, and we need to be prepared for new periods of unrest. The treatment of the year’s notification shows a broad consensus on the overall long-term strategy for the Fund’s objective. The experience of the financial crisis shows that it is important and that the fund can maintain its long-term investment strategy even in turbulent times. The objective of the State pension fund abroad is to manage the fund in such a way as to generate a long-term, good return on which future generations can benefit. The starting point for the investment strategy — whether it is investment in property, environmental investments, debt securities or shares — is for AIFMs to seek the highest possible financial return in moderate risk. The Pension Fund is managed on behalf of the Norwegian people. It is therefore important that it be managed within the values and ethical rules laid down for the Fund. In the public vocabulary about the Fund, some are concerned that the Fund’s investments will also contribute to a better environment, to the development of the emerging economies and to the development of business and jobs in the future. I would like to point out that today's investment strategy, with the ownership of many different industries internationally, an active ownership exercise, ethical guidelines and the environmental programme, is part of the fund's investment strategy and thus also contributes to this. The Ethics Council contributes to identifying sectors or areas where the risk of breaches of the Ethics Guidelines is greatest and prioritises resources to capture investments in companies that violate the Ethics Guidelines at the earliest opportunity. The work related to responsible investment practices is largely in line with the principles underlying the OECD Green Growth Strategy and UNEP – Green Economy. SPU is expected to grow significantly in the coming years. At the same time, the current contributions to the funds will decrease after each instalment. By 2020, the annual withdrawals from the Fund to cover the oil-corrected government budget deficit will be higher than the introduction of new funds into the Fund. However, the fund will continue to grow because the return on capital is expected to be higher than net withdrawals from the fund, given that the action rule is followed. The growth and size of the Fund provide a good starting point for the further development of the Fund and its investment strategy. SPU is currently a significant owner in the world stock market. SPU owns about 1 pst. of all listed shares in the world. In many companies, ownership has spread to many owners, and this makes even a possession of 1 pst. in a company, the fund is one of the largest owners. It influences ownership of the company, and therefore the ownership strategy of the fund is important. In the Communication, the Ministry prepares for the further development of the investment strategy for the SPU by 2020. New types of investment in unlisted markets such as unlisted shares and infrastructure investments in addition to the investment of property have been assessed, for which it was opened in 2010. Comparisons with other funds and the SPU’s long time horizon make it natural to assess such investments in the fund. High uncertainty about the expected return on unlisted shares and high administrative costs means that the Ministry wants to step up its efforts to make experience of investments in unlisted real estate, for which it has already opened up, before possibly moving along this track. The Ministry will come back to this question. It is supported by the majority. The Ministry has also assessed the geographical distribution of investments. The SPU is widely placed in the world’s financial markets. A new review of relevant research suggests that the foreign exchange risk in the SPU is less than previously assumed, and in any case relatively small, and that there is no longer a basis for such a strong concentration of investment in Europe as the Fund currently has. World production capacity and securities markets are more and more concentrated in other parts of the world. The Ministry therefore recommends that the proportion of the fund invested in Europe be reduced over time for the benefit of larger shares in the rest of the world. However, the European market will continue to be very important. The Ministry also mentions proposals to simplify the reference index for debt securities and any further development of the mandate, in line with the recommendations contained in the report by Schaefer and Behrens in the Communication. Such adjustments will, in the view of the Ministry, be possible without significant changes in expected returns and risks. The Ministry will consider whether it is appropriate to implement such changes during the year. Any major change in the management of the bond portfolio will be submitted by the Ministry to the Stortinget before any decision is taken, and the Ministry will report on the further development of the bond management next year to the Stortinget on the management of the fund. While foreign investment companies, parliamentarians and businesses show a great deal of interest and attention for the State pension fund, the Stortinget deals with the annual notification of the fund without the large markings. Not that we don't mean the big message about the pension fund is important – it is – but we don't make a big number out of it, and there's hardly anyone outside this room who noticed that we're handling this message today. Not so strange, maybe – it’s a little new for us to be ‘powerful in finance’, as the Minister of Industry called it in an interview last week. For both the Folketry Fund and the State pension fund abroad, well-known investment funds are in the market. The SPU has gradually become one of Norway’s leading brands abroad. Are we conscious of this, and are we taking advantage of this brand? There is a great deal of interest in the fund when the Storting Committees are on committee trips abroad or receive visits and meet politicians and others who want to hear more about the SPU and how we run the fund politically. This is confirmed to a large extent by a look-up, inter alia. Last week, today’s Industry, which tells us that other investment funds and fund managers are looking with interest to Norway and how we have posted the management of the SPU. It's not just the fact that SPU is one of the world's largest government investment funds that give the interest. It is also the investment strategy and the ethical guidelines for the management of the SPU, which have been politically initiated and adopted by the Stortinget, which are incarcerated. That's very interesting. One of the world's largest buying-in companies, according to today's economy, will follow the same line as the pension fund in terms of the framework for responsible investments. According to the paper, they believe that: ‘the Norwegian Oil Fund is a major inspiration and Norway considers that it has established a brand of competence in this area. [[...]] This is the most systematic and comprehensive approach to responsible investment we have seen so far.’ The U.S. fund wants to profile itself as the spearhead of responsible investment. That's also our political ambition for SPU. That is why we have developed and improved the ethical guidelines year after year. That is why we in ownership are concerned that fund managers should express expectations of the companies in which they invest – on good corporate governance, on prevention of child labour and the promotion of children’s rights, on risks related to climate change and water management. The fund managers have a fundamental obligation and it is to exercise an active ownership to ensure long-term, good returns on the investments the fund makes abroad, so that future generations can also benefit from the oil wealth. At the same time, a framework has been developed over time for the Fund in the form of investment strategy, ethical guidelines and a responsible investment strategy, and a competence in fund management and ethics that now makes the SPU ‘the spearhead of responsible investment’. It is a trademark of Norwegian state ownership and investment strategy, which is also underlined by the government’s ownership announcement. All parts of the Stortinget agree that SPU should not be used as a foreign policy tool. It is essential to separate SPU from Norwegian foreign policy. However, we do not exclude that SPU is a Norwegian state fund that is perceived abroad as part of Norwegian policy. According to that interview with the Minister of Industry, he also notes when travelling with business delegations around the world, that the interest in Norway is significantly stronger with the large investment portfolio we have through the SPU, and that he, for example, is In India, the SPU had been particularly well received because of its investments during the financial crisis. According to the European Manager of that American Purchasing Company, their new focus on environment, society and management will make Norway and the Nordic region even more interesting to invest in the future. Here, Norwegian industry should know its visit time and also look at SPU’s ownership strategy and ethical guidelines in the marketing of Norwegian companies and how it can contribute. It may also be interesting to look ahead to what SPU and SPN actually mean for Norwegian reputation and business today, within the objectives and framework laid down for the administration.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e389	We have spent another year with a very good return on state pension funds abroad – and inland. I would therefore like to express my initial satisfaction with the results of the fund. The national pension fund abroad, to which reference has been made, delivered a return of 9,62 pst. in 2010, of which 1.06 was pst. additional return over and above the reference index. The fund has thus taken back the poor results of the financial crisis. The State pension fund Norway delivered 15.27 pst. Last year's return, of which there was a further return of 0.56 pst. There is a broad consensus on the main features of the government pension fund management. It feels that the Right is very important to maintain and underline. There are few — if any — examples of countries that have made rapid progress in wealth, which have managed to manage it, so that it has benefited several generations without destroying the country’s economy. The broad political support – and I will also add the popular acceptance – for the way we manage our common wealth is very important to maintain. It is a responsibility not only for the political parties but also for the government and the administrators to ensure that we are able to maintain the broad support and popular acceptance. I would like to add that in this respect I believe it is important that the government, the Bank of Norway and NBIM continue to work to ensure greater transparency in the management of oil money. There I believe that the Norwegian Bank has gone in the right direction through the way to ensure greater transparency in monetary policy. There should also be a high level of emphasis in the management of pension funds. It is important to create both acceptance and understanding of the strategy and the way money is managed. It is easy to be part of the jubilee when the fund makes a lot of money. It is more demanding in times when the market falls. But the more important it is that the broad support is there, so that administrators can feel security in times that are worse than we now experience. I remember the setback in the stock market in 2002 and 2003 – and not least now in the context of the financial crisis, where the fund lost 633 billion. kr. It was then important that there was a unanimous increase in support of the strategy that had been established. It was and is a long-term strategy, and this anchoring and robustness is absolutely crucial when the fund is up in troubled times and the market is falling. I think it was strange that the rapporteur drew Stray Spetalen as a good example. There could hardly have been an example that was worse than a leading star for how to manage the fund. I remember very well March 2009 when the market was at its lowest and Stray Spetalen recommended selling in – if it was not entirely of – shares. If they had followed that advice, Norway would have been hundreds of billions of dollars poorer as a nation. I am therefore pleased that there was a large majority in Stortinget who recommended a different strategy. While the Right agrees with the main features of the government's follow-up to the management of the pension fund, we call for a more forward-looking approach to issues that naturally arise as the fund grows. First of all, this concerns the organisation of NBIM and the management of State pension funds abroad. It was right to add the administration to Norway’s Bank in its time, and Norway’s Bank has been a good home for the fund, which has reached the age of 15. But those of us who are teenage parents know that it's been a good home, doesn't have to mean that teenagers will live at home forever. NBIM has become larger than the traditional part of the central bank and requires close and competent follow-up at all levels. The right has not concluded in the way the fund and the administration will be organised in the future, but we believe it is right and necessary to explore ways of organising the fund and its management in the next phase of the fund. In the previous announcement to the Stortinget, the Ministry had a discussion of possible models. There was one possibility, which could be to set up a separate monetary policy committee, so that the Governing Council could concentrate on the follow-up of the State pension fund abroad. Another model could be a separation of the fund. The right considers that these models should be further explored, so that we can assess whether today's management is the optimal one, or whether we should choose another for the future. But let me again stress that the Right is concerned that there should be a broad political upturn and support for any changes in the way the Fund is organised. But the government should note that the four bourgeois parties are united for a proposal today, where we are asking for a further investigation. Another area where we see a slightly forward-looking attitude from the government is alternative investments, including unlisted shares and private equity. Reports have been submitted showing that there is a significant potential for exploiting the fund's characteristics as a large and long-term fund, given that the investments are being made properly. There is a wide range of seas, and it is therefore important for the Ministry of Finance to set up a robust framework for investment. Instead of addressing these demanding challenges, it seems that the government is pushing the whole issue ahead. In the middle of the big news about the management of the pension fund, we should not forget why we have created the fund. It is a design to which a whole world gives recognition, a unique way to phase large amounts into the economy in a controlled way, where it is particularly important to stress that this should not harm the competitive part of the economy. Right-wing believes it is important that we follow up the broad consensus it was when we dealt with St.meld. No 29 for 2000–2001, namely that money spent on the economy as a result of the space provided by the action rule is to be channelled into growth-creating activities, namely in infrastructure, education and research and growth-promoting tax relief. This was also one of the main points of the President of the Central Bank, Øystein Olsen's anniversary, in which he demanded: ‘Prioritisation of measures that strengthen productivity and growth capacity in the longer term, including adjustments in the tax system [[...]] I think that was well said. Finally, let me express my disagreement on another point in the speech by the President-in-Office. It is linked to the interpretation of the Minister of Finance's press release. The fact that the money in the state pension fund abroad is not earmarked for pensions does not mean that it has nothing to do with pensions. Just that we're able to build a large fund, managed in a correct and long-term way, makes today's and the future pensioners feel secure that the pension is in an account every month. At these times, how many countries in Europe are cutting down – and cutting heavily – on their pensions, and many are walking around and insecure about whether the pension is in the account next month, we can see both today’s and future pensioners in your eyes and say that you can feel secure that the pension is in your account. Therefore, State pension funds abroad have absolutely something to do with pensions. The fact that there is no earmarked pension does not undermine this important point, namely that it creates security for the pension.

It's slightly lower than the 4 pst. which forms the basis for the rule of action, but 4 pst. is, however, a reasonable estimate of the expected real return over time. History has shown that average returns may vary significantly over periods of even 10 to 20 years. If you were to change the rule every time the return turned out to be different from what we expected, we would have to change it often. A rule that often changes will lose its credibility. I guess we've all been looking at fees for single managers of 500 million. kr and such things – I think they're all over it. But the key challenge in relation to a bonus system is to design the system so that the administrator has the same interests as the fund, and that's not an easy task. When you sit down and try to figure out how to actually reconcile an administrator's interest with a fund's interest, like the State pension fund abroad, it's extremely difficult, and I look forward to the debate on how to actually design such rules. I think that's a debate that the Stortinget should have almost every year, because it's not possible to predict everything that's going on, and it's also not possible to predict all the outcomes of such an administration agreement. So there I realize that the Minister of Finance has a difficult task. I look in and around the debates about it, and hope we avoid too many suggestions of 500 million. kr to one administrator. So to the last point, it's also commented that you hired Mercer to take a look at the climate challenges. I think this is also a problem you should approach with a very big scepticism. I think there are limits to what models can actually predict, and when it comes to starting to predict how the State pension fund is going to adapt to climate change that is highly uncertain, I think you only have one alternative: to adapt to the ongoing challenges that arise when you see how climate change actually turns out. That doesn't mean you shouldn't be able to spend any money to see if it's possible to get any scenarios, but I'll say at the same time that you should keep those costs down.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e690	The Financial Market Communication is a Communication on the evolution and regulation of financial markets in 2010. Overall, this Communication provides a very useful insight into the financial markets, both nationally and internationally, and the discussion in the Financial Committee shows that there is broad consensus among the political parties here in the Chamber on the vast majority of issues dealt with. I will therefore use my time as the case rapporteur to briefly draw out some of the most important elements from the setting. Growth in the world economy rose in 2010 after the outbreak of the financial crisis. But large budget deficits and high government debt in several developed countries make the further development uncertain. The comprehensive support measures aimed at the banking and financial sectors, an active counter-cyclical policy and the effects of the automatic stabilisers have imposed very high debt burdens on many countries. Cleaning up in so-called old fun becomes an extensive and demanding job in many countries in the future. In the meantime, growth has also not been strong enough to improve the situation in the labour markets, and very high unemployment is still seen in many countries outside the borders of Norway. An overall committee is therefore concerned about the consequences of persistently high unemployment on the economy and on individuals, when, despite the decline in some countries in general, unemployment remains well above the pre-financial crisis level. It is also gratifying that a joint committee is united that, after all, we in Norway have been successful through the financial crisis. Norwegian governance quickly turned into an expansive monetary and financial policy, which in turn gave strong impetus to domestic demand. In addition, the petroleum industry has kept up and dampened the setback for the oil-related industry. We have been lucky, but the good situation we are now in – or at least a better situation – does not come about by itself, it is the result of a number of political choices. We too in Norway are facing challenges in the future. New turbulence can quickly enter the Norwegian financial system. Our best defense against international setbacks is in that context the ability to keep order in our own house in the future. An overall committee is also concerned that an increasing number of households in Norway have a debt burden that makes them vulnerable to higher interest rates, increased unemployment, or falls in housing prices. The consequences of higher interest rates may pose a threat to the economy of individuals, but also to overall financial stability, if people can no longer afford to serve their loans. Furthermore, an overall committee underlines in the nomination this year as last year, how the Norwegian deposit guarantee scheme has been important in ensuring depositor rights and stabilising deposit coverage in banks. At this point, the Committee fully endorses the government's work towards the EU in order to ensure the continuation of the Norwegian guarantee. We are also positive that the government is working for Norway to take an active part in the work that is taking place in the wake of the financial crisis by introducing new and better regulation of financial markets. Furthermore, the Committee has demonstrated in its opinion that one of the issues that has had particular international focus is issues relating to banks as so-called systemically important institutions. An overall committee considers that there is an important response to excessive risk-taking in banks internationally that infrastructure, legislation and plans are in place so that key and necessary functions in so-called insolvent financial institutions can be continued during possible liquidation. From the majority of the committee, the government parties, in addition to the Christian People's Party, we have in the vote a moment when it is pointed out that in many countries there are now discussions on how to ensure that financial institutions pay more the costs of government assistance to the banking and financial sectors. The same batches consider that this issue may also be relevant in Norway. We will come back to the discussion on these proposals when the Storting will examine the Financial Crisis Committee's report later. Finally, in the case of an incursion, two almost identical sentences have sprung into the majority's note at the very last time in Chapter 10.2, page 14 of the option. The second last sentence of the majority’s closing remark shall be deleted and it shall be deleted. With this I recommend setting.

Improvements in international financial market regulation resulting from experience from the financial crisis have an impact on the prospects for financial and economic stability. For example, the introduction of new soundness rules will include: the Basel III requirements contribute to lower risks in the international financial system, but many other improvements are unlikely to be made within several years. As a direct response to, inter alia: the representative Syversen took up, I had a meeting with my Swedish colleague early in May, where we agreed to look at the possibilities of an overall Nordic initiative in the context of the introduction of the new capital requirements in line with Basel III, and whether we are going to move somewhat faster than what is in the intention. Otherwise, there is also an international regulatory development. New rules in the context of experience from the financial crisis – back to the Basel Committee, which proposes stricter capital and liquidity requirements. The European Commission is working on the follow-up of the rules in the EU. I think one in the years to come can expect increased international cooperation in the financial sector. Financial industry is such a global and technological industry that it is important that it also sees the development of rules in a wider international context. Then I want to use a few words on the deposit guarantee scheme. As already mentioned in the debate today, the government has worked actively to enable Norway to continue its 2 million coverage. kr per customer per bank. In the recent opinion of the ECON Committee, i.e. the financial committee in the European Parliament has introduced a provision allowing Norway to maintain a level of coverage of 2 million. kr, combined with a condition that a level of coverage higher than EUR 100000 can only be offered to depositors resident in the country in question, in this case Norway, which could prevent distortions of competition. The Council shall now proceed to work on the matter. Later in the process, there will be so-called trialogue negotiations between Parliament, the Council and the Commission. It is now expected that there will be a decision after the summer, i.e. under the Polish Presidency. I had a meeting with my Polish colleague a short time ago on the matter – to keep the matter warm. I would also like to commend the Committee and the committee members for their wholehearted support, and for working against their party traps around Europe. It has contributed to the result so far. Then I would like to remind you that a 1 January of this year set up a new system of financial supervision in the European Union. Three new supervisory bodies oversee individual institutions, so-called microsupervisory bodies, while a new systemic risk board oversees the financial system as a whole, so-called macroprudential supervision. Norway, Iceland and Liechtenstein will have permanent observer posts in the micro-supervisory bodies and will be able to participate in the System Risk Board on an ad hoc basis, as it is called in the EU language, but not where individual countries or individual enterprises are discussed. I would like to stress that the government will ensure that Norway does not in any way transfer sovereignty to the new supervisory bodies. It is an interesting chapter in the financial market communication on the development of the financial industry in the last 40 years. What has happened is that this industry has grown big and significant. It is an important industry for Norway. And what perhaps not many people think about today is that the Minister of Finance not only has a responsibility for lawmaking, supervision and control, but is also the Minister of Industry for this industry. It is a task that I take seriously, considering both developing a forward-looking regulatory framework and having a good framework around the industry, but also contributing to this industry becoming a good, competitive and forward-looking industry. If one were to describe the short history from 1970 onwards, one could say it in the following way: from rationality and regulation to release, crisis and consolidation. We are now in the consolidation phase, which is important for both Norwegian and international finance industries. And the other new thing that has happened in this period is that money is no longer just money, to say it that way, but also has become a commodity and a product that in itself is to give returns and new products. So we're living in an exciting time in financial markets. In the case of the Bank of Norway and the exercise of monetary policy, the regulation is final from 29 March 2001. There is no reason to do anything else, for this, along with the rule of action, which came the same day, has served us well, and I expect this to serve us well in the future as well. Last May, Norway’s Bank increased its interest rate by 0.25 percentage points, to 2pst. The interest rate was then kept unchanged for the rest of the year. The Bank of Norway has estimated that the interest rate will increase to just over 2.5 pst. towards the end of 2011. What is important now, as I said earlier, is to take on board that both in Norway and internationally expect interest rates to increase in the future, but, of course, economic developments both in Europe – the different countries in Europe, both inside and outside the euro zone – and in Norway, will determine how much and when this happens. So a point about the State bond fund. The Public Security Fund has an important role to play in managing the government bond fund. The notification refers to a corporate event related to a bond investment that led the fund to hold shares for a shorter period. In a letter to Stortinget on 14 June, I have informed about another corporate event that is linked to a bond investment in the State bond fund. I believe it is important that the Social Security Fund has a sufficient degree of freedom to safeguard the fund's values in this kind of special situation, and I am committed to clarify this in the mandate of the State Bond Fund. Finally, a few lines about the International Monetary Fund. I would say that the IMF is important to Norway. Norway is an important supporter of the IMF, and it is also important to ensure both that the IMF has sufficient lending capacity and that the IMF governance bodies reflect developments in the international economy and changed country-to-country strength relationships. It is also reflected in the quota reform that is now taking place in the IMF. I just want to stress that Norway places great emphasis on the work of the IMF. It is an important body to ensure international financial stability in the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e1002	Norway has been through the financial crisis in a good way. The crisis was the biggest backlash in the international economy after World War II, and we now see, as many have seen, that many countries in Europe have major challenges in dealing with their sovereign debt in the wake of the major banking and financial institutions rescues that were set in motion during the crisis. Growth in the world economy has taken place after the financial crisis, but growth has not been strong enough to significantly reduce unemployment in several European countries. This creates uncertainty and unrest in many individuals and in many countries. In an interview in Today's Business yesterday, the famous investor and philanthropist George Soros said that Europe needs a plan B, and pointed specifically to the situation in Greece. He said, ‘Europe cannot let Greece default the debt because it would be an uncontrolled default with disastrous consequences for Europe’. [[...]] Norway is also part of Europe, and the countries there are our neighbours and the closest trading partners. The unstable economic situation in Europe and elsewhere in the world can easily enter the Norwegian economy, which is small and vulnerable. At the same time, one should also be alert to changes in our own economy that can contribute to creating financial instability. Many households in Norway are heavily indebted. Most of the debt is related to housing, but many households also have high consumption debts. This creates problems for the individual at home even here if interest rates rise. If many households have problems paying their interest and repayments on loans at the same time, there may also be a threat in the next round to financial stability in Norway. Good consumer protection is essential for financial stability. The financial crisis committee, which presented its position now after Christmas, has a focus on it. In recent years, and especially in the years leading up to the financial crisis, we have failed to understand many examples of sales of composite and loan-funded savings products to consumers. They prided themselves on their bank or other financial institutions' advisors, and bought products on which they lost their savings. This has now been cleared up in Norway, but there is no reason not to focus on consumer protection equally fully. We have good regulation and good supervision of the financial market in our country. For example, the financial supervisory authority seized: vis-à-vis banks and financial institutions in respect of equity requirements in respect of the purchase of dwellings. In addition, there is an obligation to discourage customers who are unable to serve their debts. In April this year, the Ministry of Finance also stressed the importance of consumer focus, with reference to the complexity of the current financial market and the ever-new product solutions being marketed. That's good. It is also important that consumers themselves become more aware of products offered in the financial sector. To make it easier for consumers, there is a financial portal that facilitates the comparison of prices and services offered to us as consumers. The financial sector itself is also committed to developing this portal, and it is important that further development takes place in cooperation with industry. Most of the players in this important sector are serious and good business operators who have the same interest as us in not getting unprofessional products on the market – products that are helping to destroy the industry’s reputation in the next round. Confident customers – loan customers or customers who want to invest their savings – are also an advantage for the industry. In recent years, we have tightened up the law in a number of areas to avoid emerging financial crises. An example is tightening up the bonus rules. It is disappointing that the Progress Party and the Right are still not in favour of the absolutely necessary tightening of the remuneration and bonus rules in order to counter incentives for high and short-term risk-taking for employees in the financial sector. It's going very well in Norway. We have good governance and control of banks and financial institutions. However, I welcome the follow-up to the Financial Crisis Committee's position, which points to a number of areas where, like other countries, we can take action to secure ourselves against new crises.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e1026	Several relations in the Norwegian economy are now pointing in the right direction. Developments in the Norwegian economy are promising the demand for goods and services in the future. It's positive. We can read in Today's Business both yesterday and today about warnings about a new financial crisis, and we also saw that Obama warned against this today. Then it is important that we put in place international rules that can help prevent a new crisis. Many countries are working on proposals for new rules and regulatory changes. The Basel Committee has introduced stricter capital and liquidity requirements for banks – the Basel III requirements. These requirements shall contribute to lower risks in the international financial system. However, it may take time for some of these changes to be made. The EU has also set up a new financial supervision system, and I believe it is unlikely that Norway should participate where we can in the new supervisory systems in the EU. I also think it is important that we have good consumer protection on the financial market. Financial supervision is responsible for consumer protection and consumer information. It's not that easy for individuals to maneuver right between all products for savings, loans, pensions, etc. available on the market. And even though entrepreneurs have an obligation to provide relevant and understandable information about the different financial products, I actually think there are many who still don't understand what they're going into. In the financial crisis selection setting, this is also pointed out. They suggest measures to make it easier for consumers. I think this is pretty serious. This is actually the world's leading economists. But it is good that the IMF now sees the responsibility, and follows up the report. And we see that as the world economy continues to improve, the IMF could move the focus from crisis management to try to learn from what has happened. Nevertheless, there is a great need for IMF loans, and the IMF has large outstanding loans. Then it is very important that the IMF should not, on an ideological basis, make demands for privatisation and liberalisation when the fund lends to states, and I am very pleased that the Left and Christian People's Party supports us in that remark. But the financial crisis has created great unemployment in the world. Over 30 million people have lost their jobs since 2007. The high unemployment rate among young people seems to me to be very serious. I can't think of anything more wasteful with resources than having young people walking without work. In Spain, the number of unemployed young people is actually up to 25 pst. I am very pleased that the IMF and the ILO will cooperate on measures to contribute to economic growth and development, and in particular to curb the increase in unemployment. The IMF has an important function that we need to support.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e1096	Several have not asked for the floor on case No. 4th@item: inlistbox [[Footing, see page 4999]]
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e1112	The matter under consideration is the result of decisions of the EEA Committee on 10 November last year which decided to amend Annex IX to the EEA Agreement to include Directive 2009/65/EC of the European Parliament and of the Council of 13 July 2009 on the coordination of laws, regulations and administrative provisions relating to undertakings for collective investment in transferable securities. The directive is also referred to as the UCITS IV Directive. To put it simply, this is the EEA passport of management companies, which provides for the establishment and management of a fund by a management company authorised and supervised in a Member State other than that in which the fund belongs. A unit trust shall be manageable directly across borders from the home Member State of a management company or through the establishment of a branch. In order for investors in funds managed on a cross-border basis not to be exposed to any additional or lower standards of protection, the Directive requires the Commission to regulate key areas such as: organisational, conflict of interest and sound conduct of business. The case also contains the decision of the EEA Committee to amend Annex IX to the EEA Agreement to include Directive 2009/110/EC of the European Parliament and of the Council of 16 September 2009 on the taking up and pursuit of the business of electronic money issuers, on the supervision of such activities and amending Directives 2005/60/EC and 2006/48/EC and repealing Directive 2000/46/EC. This means that access to the market for e-money companies will be easier and start-up capital requirements will be lower. Another key change is that the definition of e-money is made technology-neutral to take account of the likely developments. The matter may seem technical. But it's now that we're dealing more and more online, and we're using digital money today. We act across borders, and it is therefore necessary to adapt the legislation to the new everyday life we experience, and that consumer interests are also safeguarded in that kind of trade. The new e-money Directive lays down requirements for consumer protection and the repayment of paid-up capital from customers. With that I put the matter forward. The Committee has no further comments and consents to the approval of the decision of the EEA Joint Committee.

The members of the Committee of the Progressive Party, the Right and the Christian People's Party point out that a traditional government of the municipalities has been able to divide into two main dimensions: governance of the objectives or governance of the instruments. Management of the instruments in relation to municipalities has in the past been a lot of use, e.g.: by earmarking funds. Under today's government, however, we see that governance is double. However, the proportion of earmarked funds is reduced, but governance through other leaders, such as regulations, legislation and laws, has increased in parallel with government governance of objectives on behalf of municipalities, through both right-wing legislation, maximum pricing of different services and objectives such as: full child-care coverage. In recent years, the expenditure of municipalities has increased more than that of the government. This practice implies that municipalities are entrusted with tasks that are not fully financed. This is confirmed, inter alia: in PwCs and KS' R & D project: ‘Who takes the bill? What threatens this freedom is that there are no resources to develop and maintain skills-based, stable and locally adapted services in the areas considered to be the most central ones: school, health and care. Simplification from today's system to a two-tier model with only state and municipality will contribute to efficiency. The right considers that, in addition to the Quality Community Programme, the government's work on renewal in the municipal sector has stopped altogether. Municipalities are now working hard to convert and streamline their expenditure in line with the level of income. There it is that you have shaving of the public budgets, drastic cuts. The aftermath of the financial crisis is dramatic, with high unemployment — a devastating unemployment rate for youth, which prevents young people from entering and getting their first job, and which is likely to be constantly ensuring that they never get in. These are many countries that have tried recipes, from the right to the right. The more right-wing countries have been, the less good it has been for some of them during the years of building up and cutting taxes, and instead of investing in private business in a way that has not been sustainable. In contrast, Norway, a peaceful ‘island’, is all the north of Europe. We have the lowest unemployment throughout Europe. We have had increasing employment for a long time. Also this year we expect new jobs with more people doing a job. We have had immigration from other countries in Europe to contribute to that value creation and well-being. Many of them stay here when things go bad in other countries. We have a good economy in Norway because we have a red-green government that hasn't scoured away the money to tax bills, but instead invested in the future, in value creation and in welfare. Yesterday, figures from the Statistical Central Bureau showed that, during the red-green period of government, we have received over 50,000 new annual works around the municipalities, who have gone to school, who have gone to school, to kindergarten, with the giant children's garden revolution we have been through, to the welfare of both young people and the elderly who need it – to care for everyone. That's our goal: to spend the big money on care. There is no doubt that the welfare state and community investment are important in several different ways. One is that it helps balance the differences. The welfare state balances differences. Instead of investing in more tax havens, most of all for those who have the most – such as Gjermund Hagesetter was in on when he goes for a tough defense to remove the wealth tax, which will give the most to those who have the most – there is a commitment to the welfare of a cohesion policy. That is why we also see differences sinking in Norway, and have done so under the red-green government. The fact that the differences are decreasing is, in my opinion, the most beautiful thing with Norway. Society with small differences is a society where people trust each other. It's not that hard to understand, because when the differences are small, we feel in the same boat. Then we stand up for each other, and when we set up for each other, we get to know each other and we get to trust each other, and then we join the community because it benefits us all. Is there anyone in this room who thinks that hard work is voluntary? We have just a discussion about integration, where the word "dugnad" is central. Just that word you don't have in all languages, but everyone understands how to help each other, because it's rooted in most cultures. But in my homestead team, where I grew up, it was like one of my neighbors convinced me that there was a separate room in hell for those who weren't on duty. Dugnad is mandatory. For people have confidence in each other when they know each other and know each other, and people know who the rapporteur in their municipality is. Investing in a good local economy is the best way to pursue district policy. It is clear that these new jobs – over 50 000 new annual works that have come around the municipality of Norway over the last few years the red-greens have fallen – have come across the whole country. They don't just come to Oslo or Bergen, so people move and stay in the big cities. They're coming all over the country. We know that there are a lot of ladies working in public. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e1603	The municipal sector is a sector that affects all residents of Norway. We all live our lives in a municipality, and we all receive some form of municipal service. A strong public sector and common welfare arrangements are a necessary prerequisite for ensuring all equivalent services. In order to adapt policy priorities to the needs of the population, it is necessary to decentralise most services to the municipalities. The municipal sector has seen significant growth during this government — from 2005 to 2010 for a total of 48.2 billion. kr., i.e. on 17 p.m. Despite significant improvements, Kommune-Norway also has major challenges in the future. The number of older people will increase in the coming years, thus increasing the demand for services in the care sector. Through the Interaction Reform, municipalities will be given new and exciting tasks and many new skills jobs. The interaction reform shall be implemented over time from January 2012. The municipalities are transferred 5 billion. kr from the State, from health establishments, to finance the new tasks. The reform shall ensure an effective patient trajectory and good services at the lowest possible effective level. The main objective of the reform is to provide users with access to healthcare as close as possible to home. The opposition claims that the municipalities are unable to solve these problems, and one can sometimes get the impression that patients will be thrown out of the hospital from 1 January and put on the street if the municipality of origin is not ready for an offer. Of course it's not like that. Many municipalities have already expanded their offer and can accept ready-to-print patients, e.g. offer lucky day places, from 1 January 2012. Other municipalities have chosen to solve these tasks in cooperation with neighbouring municipalities and have built or are in the process of building up an intermunicipal service offer in this field. Others have not reached the same stage in the process and they will choose to purchase the services of health businesses for a transitional period. All of these solutions are equally correct. It is much more important that municipalities take time to plan, get the necessary skills in place and provide patients with an offer that is at least as good as the healthcare provider can offer before taking over the tasks. It is much more important than the fact that the offer is in place for a specific date. The government aims to enable all municipalities to offer a daily activity offer to demente by 2015. In order to ensure a stepping up of the daily activity offer, it is proposed to establish an earmarked grant that will provide 5,000 new patients with a daily offer part of the week. The daily activity offer contributes to dementing a meaningful everyday life, and in addition, it is an important relief offer for the relatives. A good and predictable relief offer allows demente to live in owner-occupied dwellings much longer. Independently of the municipal structure, there will be a great need for different forms of inter-municipal cooperation. When the co-municipal model is now legalised, municipalities will be given a new tool to choose from when it comes to the organisation of inter-municipal cooperation. It facilitates the cooperation of municipalities across all sectors under a common joint municipal authority and will be particularly suitable for municipalities that have all their inter-municipal cooperation in permanent cooperation regions. The centre party will focus on good public governance and control in relation to different forms of inter-municipal cooperation, in particular in key areas such as health and well-being. New tasks arising from the cooperation reform are, in my view, best organised either on the basis of the host municipality model with political meaning or on the basis of the joint municipal model. It is not possible to undermine the fact that municipalities face major challenges, both because of population development and because of several new tasks, but I believe that local politicians have both the ability and the willingness to perform these tasks in a thorough and good way, including in 2012. Today's document responds well to the challenges facing municipalities. Revenue is increasing, and the municipal bill provides a decent framework for good services to more and more users.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e1677	The Christian People's Party suggests that political decisions should be taken at the lowest appropriate level. This presupposes that power distribution and funding are such that both municipalities and county municipalities have the means to carry out their tasks. Municipalities are the main provider of welfare services. This requires a predictable and good local economy. There must be consistency between the tasks assigned to the municipality and the finances of the municipality. State reforms and impositions must therefore be followed up by sufficient free means, but these considerations are unfortunately not sufficiently addressed in the government’s 2012 scheme for municipalities. The Christian People's Party does not believe it is right, as indicated by the government in the municipal proposition, that the scheme for the local economy in 2012 will allow for improved service provision and continued growth beyond what demographic development, increased interest rates, pension costs and increased wages require. The reality is that for many municipalities there is a status quo, and perhaps even some municipalities unfortunately have to cut off important services such as care, school and child protection. The Christian People's Party believes that the community structure must be strengthened by 1 billion. in addition to the government's proposal, if there is to be scope for greater financial freedom. The need for increased quality of health care, schools and care for the elderly, user-driven personal assistance, resource-intensive services and child-resistance upgrades show that municipalities are badly in need of more room for action. The Christian People's Party has fought for a long time to bring about a rapprochement plan for municipal child protection. The interaction reform is a very important reform and can be described as a reversal of the health supply in Norway. The biggest change is the organisation of health services, as more responsibilities and tasks are transferred from regional health enterprises down to municipalities. I agree with the principle of the Interaction Reform that major parts of the patient course should be transferred from the specialist healthcare service to the municipal health care service, but there are a number of questions that remain unanswered. In order to meet the new tasks of the Interaction Reform, municipalities need to build up both facilities and not least competences. This will lead to large costs. Even today, many municipalities have to make economic considerations heavier than the quality and professional standard of services because of the pressure in the local economy. It will also be timely to ask: how many municipalities are really clear when the reform comes into force in less than half a year? And what happens to the municipalities that are following? It is important once again to point out that this is a reform that involves major changes in all municipalities throughout Norway — across the country. There are changes for small municipalities and there are changes for large municipalities. That is why it has been the Christian People's Party's view in this matter that such a reform must be implemented at a pace that is appropriate. Many municipalities feel that this is something that is being dragged down their heads, and it serves neither the municipalities nor the patients, nor the reform as such. Finally, I take up the suggestions that the Christian People's Party is involved in promoting in this setting.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e1944	Since the financial crisis, the municipal sector has grown more than other areas of society in our economy. The local government sector provides more important growth impulses in the Norwegian economy than the activity of households, enterprises and the rest of the country. The financial crisis hit Norwegian business hard, and production and employment fell immediately quite sharply. Local authorities received more than their natural share of the fiscal policy package — and much larger than the municipal sector in other comparable countries. Much of this growth has been financed by municipalities by increasing borrowings. In recent years, as we've been dealing with, net debt has increased as a share of income; it has increased from 20 to about 20. 40 p.m., according to the Technical Calculation Committee. In the course of the debate I noticed that the red-green parties are very pleased with employment growth and increased consumption in the local government sector as such. One is not so concerned about the effects, what benefits, what services are produced for the population. So it looks like you're using the municipal sector as an alternative labour market, you're putting the labour force perspective on it. Of course, you can do that to a certain extent, but I think in this room we have to remind each other that it is not sustainable to employ the public population when faced with the decline of economic activity in the goods-producing sector and in the rest of the world. So this is the fact that the red-green parties tend to say that the use of money in the municipal sector is investment. First of all, it's not right, too much of the increased use of money is that you blow up the cost side in terms of operations. However, where municipalities invest in fixed installations, in buildings, etc., it can be seen that there is often little profitable investment, because many of the municipal investment leads to an increase in operating costs later on. This is interesting from an overall assessment of the social economy, because the prosperity of society depends on the use of resources in such a way that capital is channelled into areas that provide returns and thus the basis for new and later growth. Any strong, loan-funded growth will have to end at some point. For the local government sector, an increase in the burden of interest and depreciation, together with a lower increase in income — from tax receipts, for example: – be what will contribute to a cover. Therefore, the leverage ratio is dangerously high. So if it doesn't threaten today, it has the potential to be dangerous in the long term. It is also a threat to future freedom of action and can actually produce negative growth impulses later on when one has to tighten up from the level of consumption to which one has resorted as a habit. The consolidation of the economy in the municipal sector could later require greater adjustments and more painful changes if not in time. Therefore, the good times should be used to convert the municipalities more fully while they have resources and can finance this conversion, without in any way affecting the welfare performance of the population. I would like to remind you that the right alternative arrangement for municipalities does not mean less money, but smarter use of money. We also want municipalities to have their operating space, which is more financed by a local business, through their own tax revenues, and not equally dependent on public transfers. It is, of course, the case — including for municipalities — that the incomes that you have and that come from productive business are safer than incomes that you depend on are transferred from others, primarily from the state. So the right-hand scheme gives you evener and safer growth. We also see that the municipalities in their conversion work are still meeting standard requirements and regulations and laws that make it impossible to do things in a new and innovative way. Here they could have needed a completely different handshake from the national Parliament, which had more critically gone through the state regulations, precisely to trigger local creativity and new solutions. Another area is that new free resources, which in the municipal proposition appear to be free, actually agree to fill old gaps in the form of underfunded reforms. New free revenues thus help to cover undervalued expenditure resulting from old orders and policies. In this context, I would like to point out that the National Assembly of the country must also take an interest in the municipal structure, where it can be seen that voluntary community mergers do not exist. Even where immediate rationalisation gains will be available locally, we have also put it up so that many of the grant schemes actually favour the municipal structure we have today, and thus do not contribute to naturalisation and to the extraction of resources, for example. bureaucracy, which could have been better used by producing welfare services for the local population. I agree with the representative of the Skei Grande that there are two aspects to the merger. It is to have professionally critical mass – we live in a more specialised society, and there are many professional environments that become too spiders in the individual municipality – but I also dare to add that if you have larger units, it is possible – if you want – to bring the administrative part down to a lower level, because with the structure we have today, there will be some duplication of the basic administration that will accompany any municipality to be operated. Finally, I would like to stay a little bit with the Interaction Reform, which is mentioned on pages 75 to 83 of the Proposition. It's a very dark shadow over the Municipality of Norway. From the supporters of the cooperation reform, municipal co-financing has been proclaimed to be the jewel of the crown. In fact, this is a pretty big experiment, which unfortunately starts on a full scale from 1 January 2012. The reform is so great that one should have progressed more gradually. Knowledge of these theoretical notions should have been acquired that municipalities can really do prevention work, so that it can be read in terms of the consumption of specialist health services, before putting this theory into practice under very unforeseen and unpredictable conditions. I would like to point out that what is essential for giving people – in our case the municipalities – responsibility for keeping households with their own budgets is that they should be able to influence their income as much as possible and not least their expenditure. However, the very idea of co-financing, where a certain proportion of the cost of specialist health services at state level must therefore be charged, is an example of the fact that municipalities will not be able to influence in any way what this reform will cost them, because it is rather guided by the government’s need for expenditure support – i.e. financing the specialist health service – than by what specific needs are actually in each municipality. We can only see that we have chosen not to listen to advice from professionals and from the opposition to take the reform step by step and acquire knowledge of how it actually works. And you just get to record that the majority wants the reform to come about when it is introduced. In addition to the build-up of debt, the very effects of a reform that does not know how to function — if properly implemented with the funding principles — will be among the main uncertainties of the local government sector in the coming years.
