Local resources and their exploitation must be the basis for the development of functional labour market areas, where jobs, housing and services are seen in the context. Exploitation of resources must have local repercussions. In order to contribute to the reduction of centralisation, the development of small cities and smaller regional centres, based on local advantages, will also be important. A larger labour market, with more opportunities, will help to reduce the number of people who have to move and allow more people to return to an attractive job. Major urban areas and major regional centres need to take care of the function of balancing population development between parts of the country. The most important thing for someone to live in is perhaps that there is a meaningful job to go to — a job that fits the qualifications and training you have. When an increasing proportion of the population has higher education, the challenges are greater in the smaller nationalised areas of the country, where industries with lower requirements for formal education have traditionally been run. It contributes to the trend of centralisation and contributes to the maintenance of the biased distribution of income in a centre-periphery dimension. This is one of the reasons why redistribution is an important mechanism, which we have also strengthened by reducing the tax share of municipal income. Also in the future people will live throughout the country, in many municipalities, and everyone has requirements for equivalent services. There shall be no difference between basic welfare services in care, kindergarten or school. One of the main factors in ensuring this equivalence is skilled and competent staff in all municipalities throughout the country. In my opinion, there is reason to be concerned when there are still municipalities with one or fewer courses in child protection, which do not cooperate with other municipalities. It is at the expense of the stability and quality of services of some of the people who most need a good service. During the consultation in the committee, the Norwegian Nursing Association pointed out that small professional environments are a very central reason for getting nurses wanting to work in nursing homes and in home services in the municipalities. During the same consultation, Norsk Vann expressed concern about the lack of engineers in the municipalities. They receive support in NOU 2011: 3 Competence jobs – driving growth across the country. The sample points to the fact that many municipalities have small and vulnerable trade unions, and that this may affect the quality of services and hamper recruitment. I therefore agree with the Council of Ministers of Navarsete when she pronounces to the Bureau on 14 March this year: ‘The objective must be to make municipalities even more robust and attractive as jobs.’ When the municipal committee travels around many of the country's municipalities, we note that discussions about the municipal structure are taking place. In several places where this is discussed, economic incentives are sought to ensure real volunteering by pooling. The Labour Party believes that this is the basis for listening to and discussing the proposals that come up with regard to how this can be done. The above-mentioned sample also points to the lack of such incentives for voluntary community mergers at present. For the Workers' Party, it is essential to ensure robust, functional and sustainable municipalities – not because we have to save money, but because both the Stortinget and the local population are increasingly demanding what municipalities should do in child protection, in schools, in kindergartens, and in spatial planning, preparedness and much more. In short, the Labour Party will have functional and sustainable municipalities, because we need to strengthen, improve and renew the welfare offer, not build it down.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e2459	It is well known for the most part that in the Progress Party we have worked for a long time to abolish the county municipality as a self-governing level. This is the first task that concerns or has an impact on a larger area than the individual municipality, and therefore needs overarching solutions. Secondly, for reasons of economies of scale, tasks are best solved with a larger population base than most municipalities have. And thirdly, there are tasks that require more resources in terms of economics and specialised competence than the individual municipalities have at their disposal. All these tasks that need to be seen in a wider perspective than the level of the municipality cannot be transferred to the municipalities. Lowering the county will in reality mean that tasks will be transferred from a regionally elected level to the regional central government. There won't be much local democracy of it. This cannot be said to strengthen local democracy.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e2510	The proposers call for more power to be decentralised and for tasks to be handled as closely as possible to the citizens and therefore want to liquidate the county as a self-governed level of government. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e2644	The President cannot see that someone has asked for the floor on this matter. – The representative Michael Tetzschner.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e2649	President, I have signed up as rapporteur of the case. I just want to add a few comments to the matter. Firstly, it is a pleasure to be helpful to the Ministry of Justice that Hans Tranekjer Andresen can be employed as an official in the Ministry of Justice. It's probably a wise appointment. Just a few words to general information on why the matter is here today: it is the case that the Constitution § 92 operates with certain subsidiarity requirements for citizens who, in addition to being residents, also have to be civil servants. It is not enough to be a citizen, you also have to have a right of origin. And you get that either by being born here and raised here, or, to satisfy the demands of being employed as an official, you have lived in the kingdom for at least ten years. Tranekker Andresen has not. It is the defect – if you can call it it – that the Great Britain then has to deal with, by going to what is called naturalisation, and it is precisely that one can deviate from native law as an additional requirement in addition to citizenship. I can also remind you that this is a provision that has its roots in the legislation before the Constitution of 1814. It was adopted as a regulation in 1776, 15 January, where there were also subsidiarity requirements in addition to citizenship in order to hold office. So it's like we're giving birth rights with this, and we've solved the Justice Department's problem. Then perhaps those of this assembly's constitutionally interested people will reflect further on whether this is a kind of rule that has survived itself.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e2662	I should like to thank the President-in-Office for two reasons. One is that it was a thorough and particularly informative review of the matter. The second concerns the invitation to reflect a little on the provision in the light of the matter. Like the rapporteur, I am pleased that naturalisation has been proposed for the person concerned, as it is up to the Storting to do. But when such a case – which does not often appear in the Stortinget – is here, I think there is reason to point out that there may be a basis for assessing whether the provision, which the rapporteur had in mind, has survived. We kind of have a discussion about degrees of being a Northman – to what extent is a Northman? It goes on a scale, from having a residence permit here in the country, through your being granted citizenship, which we are discussing in some context, and now also to being a question of whether you have a right of birth or are naturalized a Norwegian. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e3126	The requirement for equal pay for women and men has long traditions. The first demands for equal pay in industry were made by female workers in the 1880s and 1890s. It would be 80 years for the LO and Norwegian Employers' Association to enter into a framework agreement on equal pay between women and men in 1961. In 1947, the Ministry of Social Affairs took the initiative to monitor women’s and men’s wages, and it showed, among other things, that they were able to do so. that it was difficult to defend the pay gap between 20 and 40 pst. depending on family circumstances and depending on the burden of living. In 1949, the Equal pay Commission was set up, with a mandate to both give an overview of women’s wages and salaries in the labour market and discuss how inequalities could be corrected. In 1951, the ILO Convention on Equal Pay for Women and Male Workers was adopted for work of equal value, and the majority of the Committee voted in favour of ratification from 1958. Finally, in the EU, the principle of equal pay was incorporated into Article 119 of the Treaty of Rome. The reason was to prevent distortions of competition. With the long story, and the tradition of fighting for equal pay, I'm glad that the proposers are concerned with what is a struggle for the movement of workers, but we disagree with the medicine they prescribe. The Equality Act provides in Section 5 that women and men in the same undertaking should have equal pay for equal work and work of equal value. The remuneration shall be determined in the same way for both women and men and shall apply irrespective of whether the work belongs to different trades or whether the remuneration is regulated in different collective agreements. The value of the work shall be determined on the basis of an overall assessment focusing on the competences necessary to carry out the work and other relevant factors, such as: effort, responsibilities and working conditions. So pay is not a relevant factor. When the Progress Party wants to intervene politically in the determination of wages, it is attacking one of the foundations of the Norwegian social model. An organised working life with a strong element of central negotiations is one of the most important contributions to a low level of conflict, high employment and relatively small income differences. Wages and salaries are the responsibility of the parties, as is also initially shown in the context of the proposal. In the state tariff area, women have an average of 94 pst. of men's statutory wages and salaries. Adjusted for age, education, work experience, seniority and working time, women have 98–99 pst. of men's statutory wages and salaries. The parties to the State tariff area have good processes to reach an agreement on measures to balance the pay gap between women and men, and we assume that the same is true in the other areas of public sector negotiation. Of course, we also expect the private sector to follow up on its own. There is an overall committee, with the exception of the Progress Party, which supports the position on this matter.

This is a matter which we think is important and which we think should be debated. Of course, it is quite right that the pay settlement is initially the responsibility of the parties, but here we believe that it is possible that the government will come and help the parties so that one can get anywhere in this matter. But in 2009, there was a campaign, and then it made a sense that something was going to happen, but it didn't. As we refer to in the suspension, the Committee on Gender Equality and Discrimination stated in the hearing organised in connection with the treatment of the equal pay report by the Stortinge, that the government must contribute fresh funds to women-dominated professions in the public sector and that the parties must commit themselves to prioritizing this group in the future. The Christian People's Party fully agrees with this. In our society, it is largely the family that is the foundation. Therefore, it is essential that the labour market be adapted to family life and not the other way around. The family must be at the centre to ensure safe growth for the smallest and most vulnerable. It should not come to that point that the young people, the time spent with young people and leavers are an obstacle to equal pay and a good working life. We know that many women become dependent on income as a consequence of births and leave. It's a problem. This problem is not solved by women discontinuing or discontinuing leave. No, the solution must be in the workplace. Part of the solution may be found in the majority proposal of the Equal Pay Commission that workers in parental leave should be entitled to average wage growth. The Christian People's Party believes the fair thing would be that everyone got to participate in the wage development that happens at their workplace while they're on leave. Such an automatic right to pay adjustment can be an important instrument to balance the negative pay effects of parental leave. If we ensure that workers do not suffer from impaired pay and working conditions as a result of taking parental leave, we have come a little further on the road to equal pay. In St.meld. No 70 for 1991-1992, the government of Harlem Brundtland wrote that the main unresolved problems were equal pay and equal care. The Communication stated that the main work had to be done at the workplace and through the social partners and their organisations. The reason why I mention this is that it supports whether the Christian People's Party is so busy in this area. It is not with changes in family life that equal pay challenges are to be solved. Changes in the labour force are the most important. There are structures in society that prevent gender equality. It is crucial for Norwegian families that employers and society in general facilitate a good family life, and that it is possible to combine careers, children and families in a good way. Unfortunately, despite considerable attention over time, equal pay challenges are still high, and we still have a job to do.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e3229	Equal pay is important for the government, but wages and salaries are the responsibility of the parties. Also in the KS, Spekter and Oslo Municipality there was a special focus on equal pay in the main tariff settlement in 2010. The parties have shown that they are able to find solutions to the pay gap between women and men within a sound financial framework. It's about prioritizing. However, it is important to focus on equal pay issues also on full-time and part-time issues, to have good welfare arrangements, and not least to focus on education and career choices. The fact that we have such a sex-segregated labour market in Norway contributes to creating the gender pay challenges we face, and it is therefore important that we encourage girls to make untraditional career choices. It will help balance pay differentials.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e3314	In recent years, privacy has been widely debated in Norway. On 4 April, Stortinget held a wide-ranging debate on the implementation of the EU Data Storage Directive, with both the minority and the majority focusing on the best possible protection of privacy. The case rapporteur made a good point; I can join everything he said. The proposal we are discussing today points to a number of areas of society where there is reason to be concerned with people's right to privacy, transparency and ownership of personal data. The proposal was promoted before Stortinget dealt with the Data Storage Directive, and many of the issues addressed were therefore addressed in the negotiations and the agreement concluded between the Workers' Party and the Right. In the agreement, we focused on an overall approach to privacy, and on finding balanced solutions, solutions that balance the principle of the right to privacy with society’s need for regulation. The government has notified a major privacy announcement. I'm glad. It is important that the Stortinget has the opportunity to discuss privacy in general. Different factors, in particular technological developments, challenge privacy. Storage of personal data, both for private operators and for the public, is becoming increasingly easier – a large amount of data can be stored, and it is often cheaper and easier to keep the data stored than to delete it. It may also be close to using data stored for one purpose for another. It is a responsibility of the authorities to ensure the necessary counterweight to this development through legislation that imposes erasure and requires a solid justification for all the storage of personal data by ensuring the individual right to know what is stored through supervision. If we are to ensure the privacy of people, the public must take an active responsibility. At the same time, the state is also an actor. It goes without saying that the state itself must also be careful about collecting, storing and compiling personal data. With regard to the specific content of the original proposals of the individual representatives from the Right, something is already part of today's legislation, other is current practice. In addition, it has been announced that several of the proposals will be discussed in the Storing Communication. I will therefore carefully comment on a few of the proposals promoted by the Progress Party and the Right, and which will be voted on here today. In the case of tax lists, there is something Storting has discussed several times. In January the Ministry of Finance sent out a proposal for tightening the way tax lists are published. The proposal will limit the commercial use of tax lists. It will be an important contribution to strengthening privacy. Proposal No. 2 is in line with current practice. It appears from the State Council's reply to the Committee that toll companies do not provide access information to the equality authorities and that the government will consider limiting the possibilities for such transparency. I would like to welcome such an assessment, but at the same time I would like to stress that organised crime and a significant black economy are a major problem across Europe. Finally, I would like to comment on a general issue that I believe we need to come back to. At first sight, it makes sense to log or log out who collects information from public registers. But is it not a paradox that the desire to strengthen data protection calls for the establishment of a number of new registers of personal data? To give an example, if the public is to register all applicants for Kjell Inge Smokes and Jens Stoltenberg's tax information, as the Right Party and the Advance Party wish, a new personal data register will be established at the same time – a list of whom and when and why. It's not unproblematic. Could it not be that the information contained in this new log is as sensitive as that contained in the original tax lists? And isn't it the case that two public registers double the risk of data being diverted? And how to log who reads the log – do you see a third register, the log of the log? It seems a convenient solution to ensure that everyone who retrieves personal data from public registers is recorded. It's a little bit of a thoughtful suggestion. In my view, you risk the very opposite of what is intention, a weakening of privacy.

It is the individual who has to bear the burden of system failure, and when the systems fail, we hear that it is not really the technical of those who have failed, but the human by them, and that it was humans like slumped and forgotten memory sticks or other storage media, in peril or by direct disloyalty. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e3398	This case clearly shows the extent of privacy. Privacy is cross-sectoral and the proposal ranges from data protection measures in the health sector through the publication of data on social protection data. Almost no matter what we do, we leave personal data that is processed in different contexts. We pay with different electronic means of payment in public transport, in the bombing and in the store. We use mobile phones and computers many times, in different places, in one day. We visit doctors and dentists, we borrow books from the library, and we use public services. As freedom of expression and legal certainty, privacy is an ideal interest – difficult to measure in crowns and ears. Nevertheless, privacy must be weighed against other interests. The importance of efficiency, road safety, good health and crime prevention, in order to mention something, must be weighed against the interests of citizens' privacy and privacy as well as society's desire for a limited level of control and surveillance. In order to ensure the diversity of societal interests, it is necessary to find solutions that least interfere with the personal sphere of interest of citizens. We must maintain one social interest without sacrificing another. We must work for good privacy and privacy-friendly solutions in both private and public businesses. In relation to logging, this means that priority must be given to developing good systems to prevent unauthorised access to personal data. We need to recognise that errors can happen, and that some people can abuse their legitimate access to information to search for information they do not need. This is a log tool that can help to protect the interests of data subjects when errors are corrected. Another data protection solution may be that until the data subject receives a copy of the personal data, which is provided to the external data subject. This has been the right in the context of credit information for many years. At the same time, we need to think carefully before imposing the obligation to copy information from all public registers, because it can quickly become a large amount of information for data subjects to deal with. With regard to the specific obligation to copy the tax lists, this has recently been assessed in connection with a law that I understood was dealt with in this Chamber only a few days ago. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e3468	It is a pleasure for me to present the position of the Business Committee on the agricultural settlement of the year. I would like to thank the members of the Committee for good cooperation, so that we could properly handle the settlement here in Stortingen. The framework of the arrangement is 1420 million. kr, which is financed by SEK 860 million. SEK in increased target price and SEK 365 million. kr in grants from the State budget. The remainder of the coverage has been transferred from 2010 onwards and increased value of the agricultural deduction. One important element of this year’s settlement is that the government proposes to remove the food production fee in the 2012 government budget. It will provide a tax relief of 280 million. kr for Norwegian agricultural products with effect from 1 January, which is the basis for the agreement of the parties on target price increases from the same date. But for industry, this means a total tax return of over 600 million. kr. And I have to say that I am a little surprised that we have not heard more cheers from either industry or the opposition that this strange fee on food production is now falling away. The agreement also requires that agriculture now has to pay a full membership fee to the population, i.e. the termination of co-financing through the agreement. It is compensated for by the corresponding revenue-generating measures under the agreement and by the increase in the agricultural deduction within a proceeds framework of approximately 120 million. kr. It is worth noting that non-agricultural forest users do not benefit from this, and there is therefore a consensus to increase the forestry and bioenergy reserve over the agricultural development fund as a partial compensation. This agreement continues the stepping-up of opportunities for increased profitability in agriculture as started in 2006. The strong district profile of last year’s settlement is continued but not further reinforced. Grain production is a priority in this settlement, in order to underpin the need for food and feed grain to be produced on the best areas of ‘historically good grain settlement’, which the head of Eastfold Bondelag stated at the time of the agreement. Yes, it is worth noting that increased cereal production is stimulated by an increase in the area grant, compensation is paid for increases in fertiliser prices, an increase in the target price for food and feed grain, an increase in the price write-down subsidy and an increase in the allocation of local cargo subsidies for feed. It is also agreed to introduce a subsidy for the stockpiling of cereal seed. That's very good. The channelling policy is an important mechanism to ensure that food and feed grain is produced on the best areas, and milk and meat produced in the districts. Then we get the most out of our total resources. And when the grazing industry becomes a priority, as we have done since 2006, we also get to maintain the cultural landscape as an effect of food production. I think this is a very wise step in our agricultural policy to ensure that we have production and housing across the country. On the basis of agriculture, we have a decentralised housing and employment structure, and I believe that we have done well in Norway. And yet, too many uses are empty, and in my view, we have too low land productivity on part of our area. There's a lot to do here. So, of course, if we're to maintain an agriculture all over the country, we have to realize that we need to have a varied usage structure, adapted to the different framework conditions that nature and geography provide. Nor will we succeed without the willingness to use budgetary resources as well as the need for agriculture to take a significant share of the income from the market. If we are to succeed in maintaining self-sufficiency as a growing population, we must take advantage of all the opportunities we have, and we must have robust customs protection. Here, perhaps, we are at one of the most demanding agricultural policy balances. Farmers must have sound profitability in their productions, while at the same time maintaining commodity prices at a level that the food industry can defend, and we are dependent on importing a number of inputs. We also have a duty on duty-free and quota-free access to the Norwegian market for all products from countries on the UN MUL list, which was launched from 1 July 2002. There are now 64 countries that can export all their agricultural products to Norway without customs duties. These imports have been slowly increasing in recent years. As an example, it is interesting to mention our imports of beef from Swaziland. The government opened there for an import quota of 500 tonnes of beef a few years ago. Here there is now a positive development going on, which is very important to small farmers in Swaziland, which I have registered myself during a visit. As far as the agricultural economy is concerned, the Labour Party believes that the challenges are greatest in milk production. Here also comes the heaviest investment needs. The way in which production will be organised will vary from use to use and from area to area. Collaborations can be an excellent way to organise production in too many, mainly where there is little use, while in other areas it is preferable to expand on the basis of additional purchases and renting of milk quotas. But this will vary a lot. It is important that the user himself makes these adjustments on the basis of his own competence, economics and local opportunities. I'm not in favour of us being politicians driving and musing for one way or another to organize production, but it's important that we, as politicians, set things right, so that the farmer can take the right choices from his own point of view. The Committee has drawn up a note on the situation in beef and veal production and underlines the need to continue to stimulate suckler cows. Ammeku has become an important element of Norwegian livestock farming and meets the need for grazing. It is a good alternative to sheep in predatory areas, while the open cultural landscape is also positive for maintaining biodiversity. Otherwise, it's hard to talk about grazing without addressing the challenges we have with predators in some of the grazing areas. Although this is a matter for another committee, it has so much to do with the opportunities to engage in industry in some districts. I very much hope that the efforts now being made at the parliamentary level must lead to a broad settlement, a settlement which, at my discretion, should lead to the establishment of more efficient predatory management. Then, of course, grazing has to be done to avoid grazing livestock being exposed to predators to a greater extent than is necessary. The grant for organised grazing has also been increased in this agreement. The two parties' budget proposals do not bear witness to this. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e3541	The party regards self-employed farmers as a support bar for the creation of value and nutrition in many places in the country. We want the farmer to have development opportunities in the future. A robust agricultural industry contributes to achieving important objectives of continued significant Norwegian food production, differentiated housing and beautiful cultural landscapes. We believe that agriculture, like, for example. the tourism industry and the fishing industry are of great importance in ensuring positive development in many communities. We record that the agricultural conversion continues. It is estimated that 36 000 farms with housing are empty, many of them are in decline. Conversion to larger and more efficient units is important and necessary to ensure that the agricultural industry becomes more forward-looking and less dependent on transfers, and we believe that agricultural policy should better facilitate this. In principle, it is a concern that individual industries negotiate their own framework conditions directly with the State and conclude agreements with major economic consequences outside the ordinary budgetary procedure. The annual decisions on the framework conditions for agriculture are not a pay bill but a determination of the possibilities for generating income. The significant deviations between the promised agricultural income development in the individual settlements and the achieved development show that the basis of calculation used for the settlement is rather thin. The Progress Party wants farmers, as other traders, to be responsible for their own value creation in accordance with the framework conditions set by society, and it is important to give agriculture new opportunities to develop and reduce dependence on public support. In order for the settlement to be seen in the context of other policy priorities, I think it is neat that the settlement is dealt with in the context of the government budget and follows the calendar year. The State shall continue to grant financial support to agriculture, but such grants must be more targeted and less bureaucratic than they are today. The Progress Party considers it central to strengthen the farmer's ownership rights for his own use, for example. to propose the removal of a dwelling obligation. Occupancy is a barrier to the future development of agriculture and forestry in Norway, and it is not proven to contribute to settlement. In many cases, price regulation provides artificially low prices and prevents turnover and increases the risk of investments on the farm. A high distance between the market price and the regulated price also provides a basis for cases where parts of the purchase fee are hidden from the authorities. We believe it is a serious interference in ownership that the state forces people who want to sell the farm to take a lower price than the buyer is willing to pay. The Party of Progress is strongly critical of the agricultural policy pursued by the Red-Green Government and cannot therefore in any way support the negotiated agricultural agreement. The new agreement does not provide for a new direction in agricultural policy, despite the fact that there is a great need for a change of course in agricultural policy. Despite the fact that the red-green government announced a strong change in agricultural policy when it came to power, this has not happened. This year, too, there was a big gap between the requirement submitted by the farmers' organisations and the offer that came from the state. The negotiated agreement is approximately half of the initial requirement of the organisations. It is necessary to establish a normal market-based system for agricultural products managed by supply and demand. In the case of under-coverage in the market, the producer will be able to take a higher price, while the consumer can choose other products if the price of a product is too high. In that case, the prices of the products will vary in line with the market and the producers must be market-oriented in their production. In a situation that today, where consumers can demand more than the Norwegian farmer can produce, a market-based system will contribute to higher production. In a market-based system, competition between manufacturers will arise, leading to better products, innovation and more cost-effective production. This will contribute to better income opportunities for capable, innovative producers who are good businessmen. The Party will point out that market-based systems operate in all other markets and that there is therefore no reason to exempt agriculture from this. The sharp decline in the number of farmers and use will continue in the coming years and probably with increased force. Agricultural actors have little confidence in the future, and the confidence in the government's agricultural policy is at a low level. The agricultural policy that has been pursued for many years is a socialist experiment that has contributed to the Norwegian farmer being caught up in a network and a system that is now almost impossible to get out of. Agricultural policy is characterised by a strong drive to regulate the industry down to the smallest detail and by the fact that public bureaucrats and very detailed legislation have taken over the direction of agricultural development. The management to be provided by individual farmers over their own property and employment has disappeared, and the farmers are now more to be regarded as public employees and practitioners of a public policy than as self-employed. This has led to a lack of creativity, ability to invest and a desire to take risks in large parts of agriculture. I find it strange that both the agricultural organisations and the Central Party support a policy that leads to fewer farmers and much dissatisfaction with the income situation and opportunities among industry actors. It is therefore important to point out that the agricultural contract system has become so complex and complicated that few are outside the central bureaucracies of the Ministry of Agriculture and Food that are able to get involved in all aspects of the system and fully understand it. Nor have any other OECD countries found this appropriate. This may be harmful to other export industries, including: fish, when you put a milk toll on the fading 436 pst. Norway has to contend that international developments will reduce import protection in the wake of the WTO negotiations. The development offers a gradual increase in competition for Norwegian agricultural products. Despite a high level of customs protection, the Norwegian authorities have not set out a plan for meeting such a trend in Norwegian agriculture. This is very critical. The result may be that Norwegian agriculture will have a blue day when the outcome of the WTO negotiations becomes known. We need a new development of robust production, based on market economy. Some time ago, the food selection provided the analysis ‘Food, Power and Power’, which was to say something about the power conditions in the food value chain. Unfortunately, this became a political ordering agency that did not include those who really control food prices in the country, namely the monopolists and market regulators Tine and Nortura. Nearly nothing works in the Norwegian defunct, socialist agricultural policy. We have the world's most expensive food, with the world's worst selection, something agricultural minister Lars Peder Breck is brilliantly pleased with. Effectiveness cannot be achieved when the market is governed by a regulator and a monopolist who controls everything, such as: in the chicken industry. Rigid rules make, among other things, that chicken production has to be shut down for much of the year. Ole Robert Reitan told the following story at a lunch meeting, organized by Civita, about a meeting with the monopolist and regulator Nortura: He had first met with chicken producers, who were pleased that they had increased the hatch percentage so that profitability went up. Of course, it's important for a farmer to get the hatch percentage, because that's where the income comes, and that's when he gets more meat to sell. This he told Nortura at a meeting, who commented that it was not desirable for Nortura, because then more eggs were produced, and the price would be lower. If we go back to November last year, Nortura had a huge surplus of pork. But then the chains started the price war on ribs. What happened? In two weeks we were free of ribs in Norway and had to reduce customs protection in order to import ribs from other countries, while the government allowed the Norwegian rib to be dumped in Ukraine to less than 10 kroner per kg. Market regulator Nortura had exported — or dumped in good Norwegian — nearly 2 000 tonnes of pigmeat. The Minister of Agriculture's reaction to the fact that most people could avoid a little bit of cheap ribs was that, on questions from the undersigned from the Parliament's pulpit, he said that he thought it was 'utic' to pay less than 14 pounds for the pork meat. Where is the ethics when the policy of the Minister of Agriculture leads to the dumping of meat in Ukraine for well under 10 pounds and not 14 dollars? The government of Stoltenberg is clearly more concerned with subsidising cheap food in Ukraine than with pursuing a proper, good agricultural policy. It's not exactly a consumer-friendly policy we're looking at from the government today. This is socialist policy at its best – giving the world cheap meat and letting Ola and Kari Nordmann pay the bill. We also hear that from the representative of the Workers' Party here, who tells us how great it is to import from Swaziland. The food in Norway is 30-40 pst. more expensive than in Sweden and Denmark. Customs protection is responsible for a lot of this. It gives us poor samples, and it also gives us the world's most expensive food. We have a border trade that, according to the SSB, is 10.5 billion. kr, which according to the Swedish Trade Institute has reached a staggering 20 billion. kr. So what's right, and what's wrong? I think we have to assume that maybe the SSB has a little bit too low when you see what others come with. In Norway, food production needs to be increased by 20 pst. by 2030 to give birth to one million more Nordics. We politicians must take that responsibility internationally by ensuring greater self-sufficiency of Norwegian food based on Norwegian raw materials. It is then important that, through the agricultural policy and general framework conditions, we lay down conditions that require increased food production. And as we hear in this room, we still have a way to go politically before we can agree on these challenges.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e3988	The agricultural agreement that Stortinget is dealing with today is, in my opinion, a good deal. This year, too, the government, through negotiations, has ensured the necessary growth in the income opportunities for its food producers. In doing so, we are continuing the important change in the course of the government's agricultural policy on the day that the red-green government took over in 2005. Income levels and developments are the main basis for ensuring recruitment and investment. By allowing the industry, which consists of self-employed people, to have opportunities to increase its incomes, we also make the industry even better able to deliver what the large community orders: agriculture throughout the country, rural settlements, cultural landscapes and safe Norwegian food. When the red-green government took over, agriculture was in the midst of a very negative development. Low income trends and thus considerable uncertainty about recruitment, as well as a lack of the necessary investment will, led the industry into a negative spiral. The red-green government has reversed this trend. That was absolutely necessary. And of course we haven't done it in any way. We'll go even further. A government that shows a willingness to invest in agriculture meets high expectations in industry, sometimes very high expectations. This became particularly evident when Norwegian Farmers' and Small Farmers' teams broke the negotiations this year. I am pleased that the Norwegian Bond team chose to enter into an agreement and use the influence the negotiations provide the industry. This year’s agreement raises the net income per farm per year by approximately NOK 25 000 in 2012 compared to the level calculated in 2011 before the negotiations. That's more than 10 pst. increase. And I want to stress that we're talking about net income here. The NOK 25 000 comes after a forecast cost increase of SEK 735 million. kr is covered in. This means that it is compensated for a cost increase of 3 pst., for total agricultural costs is almost 25 billion. kr, including interest payable. The agreement also takes into account that the revenue trend from 2006 to 2011 was estimated to be lower than that of the parties to the agreement last year. This agricultural agreement also contains an important new approach to agricultural and food policy. In order to ensure a strong and innovative food industry in Norway, we cannot in the long run increase the income potential of the raw material in a value chain. We're in competition with the outside world. Imports are increasing, and the whole sector needs competitiveness. As a result, the government chose to set aside the 2012 food production fee as the premise for the negotiations. By doing so, the food industry has met one of its main requirements to the authorities. Over 600 million. NOK in fees need not be paid. The Norwegian agricultural produce accounts for about 280 million. NOK of this. The rest of the fee will apply to raw material from the fishing industry and necessary imported raw materials that we do not produce ourselves in Norway. There has been a special Norwegian exercise in taxing food production. From 2012 it's gone. As we increase commodity prices at the same time, we are effectively helping to ensure that the price increase can end up with the farmer and not in other stages. It can and should contribute to a better distribution of income in the value chain. And in that way, it was possible to increase agricultural prices without harming consumers. The price increase for consumers, as a result of this year’s contract in isolation, is estimated to be 0,7 pst. average. It is therefore strange when the Progress Party says in the vote that again it is ‘the consumer who has to take the bill for the government’s failed agricultural policy’. In fact, prices have increased more in Sweden and Denmark than in Norway in recent years, and consumer prices have fallen by 0.6 pst in the last 12 months. in Norway. So I see no reason to dramatise the impact of this agricultural agreement on consumers, although the competition challenge for the Norwegian food sector is always an important consideration of agricultural policy. This agricultural agreement also gives high priority to rural agriculture, in particular milk production, which has a high investment need and is a backbone in rural agriculture. At the same time, cereal production also clearly improves the framework conditions. It makes sense to ensure Norwegian production of food and feed cereals. On the right, their criticism is partly based on what they call ‘full investment drought’. I should like to hear what is the basis of fact for that statement. Investments are now at a higher level than in the early 2000s. But it is true that investment needs are very high in the future. Therefore, improved income opportunities are also essential. And then the opposition's alternative budgets and alternative agricultural policies are not the way to go. It will provide less investment, poorer recruitment and weaker foundations for continued agriculture across the country.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e4356	I am pleased that the representative Svein Flätten in his main post said that it is important not to talk the industry down – he should. Furthermore, I would like to point out that in one of its comments, the Progress Party refers to the opinion of the Fisheries and Aquaculture industry to the NRK on 7 June 2010 that market access for Norwegian fish to the EU will be significantly lower after the introduction of a percentage duty on drinking milk. Now we have the facts, and that is that the percentage of milk – as we have the full opportunity to do under the WTO agreement – has not had any negative effects on Norwegian fish exports or other industries. This is therefore a very good example of unrooted propaganda in reality, which the Progress Party and some other social actors often stand for in the agricultural debate. So to the import protection. The protection of imports is, and will continue to be, a pillar of Norwegian agricultural policy. Import protection is a prerequisite for: to maintain production and ensure the provision of Norwegian agricultural products to ensure the level of income for agriculture; It can be illustrated by the milk prices in some selected countries, for example: Germany kr 2.55 per kg, Denmark kr 2.66 per kg, UK kr 2.17 per kg. If milk duties are removed, there will be a large import of milk from our neighbouring country, unless Norwegian prices are drastically reduced. Price reductions will in turn directly affect the income of Norwegian dairy farms and lead to dramatic reductions in consumption. Similarly, in the meat sector as a whole, customs duties on meat products will be removed. The meat prices of Norwegian farmers are much higher than in the EU and on the world market, and the loss of customs duties will lead to a dramatic increase in imports and correspondingly reduced Norwegian production. The loss of customs duties will affect both the farmer as a commodity producer and the food industry. Loss of import protection will lead to a dramatic reduction in Norwegian agriculture and the food industry, particularly in the districts.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e4753	I thought now I'd spend my 30 minutes, so we'd get a debate over and over again. The 2010 Annual Report of the Ombudsman’s Department of Defence shows a broad range of activities within the instructions and mandates given to it by the Stortinget. The name shall be the representative of the defence personnel, but it shall be equally important for those responsible for the first-time duty. A rapid review of the case shows that it may be especially boys and girls who are in first-time service, and in so far as also trained soldiers or branch agencies, who have performed first-time service, who use the Ombudsman’s name but also command and civil servants use the name. In 2010, the Ombudsman dealt with 107 registered cases. In addition, there are a number of requests where advice and guidance have been provided. Both the management of the defence and the employees' organisations agree that changes to the working situation are necessary. Official organisations still believe that the problems that exist are due to an imbalance between tasks and resources. The Ombudsman has commented for several years that the long-term phase of conversion is perceived as an additional burden on staff – and we understand that. The conversion also means that many people have to move, and I also think we should have respect for that. Nevertheless, the committee believes that the working environment in the defence sector is largely good. So a couple of things that apply to the soldiers in first-time duty. There are issues related to security clearance for guardians that seem to take a very long time. Here the Committee calls on the Ministry of Defence to take a particular look at this issue in order to avoid delays and, of course, to ensure the necessary security. The balance sheet is there, and we ask that we look at it separately. The second is guidelines for the implementation of equal terms of service, which were last revised in 2002 and launched in March 2003. The national authorities of the Security Council have taken note of the fact that more data subjects are subject to the obligation to provide services far beyond what is described in the Guidelines. The Committee agrees that the guidelines need to be reviewed and that the relevant guidelines need to be clarified. There are still too few women in Norwegian defence. I don't think there's been any particular increase in the last decade. I think there's still something over 7 pst., and it really was in 2001. But there is a slight increase in both girls taking first-time service, and in the employment of foreign personnel, so-called branch ladders, who have gone through the first-time service in advance. The Ombudsman concludes that women’s staff are well placed in the military environment, even if there is enough time left here. Then there's an area called the denominator, where there's a need to come up with some measures. The defence has problems in recruiting apprentices, while many young people on professional lines do not have apprenticeships. This mismatch has to be done with. I have received input from several sides, such as: Why not integrate the duty of protection into apprenticeship for students on professional lines? So that thought has also been passed on. The case is also important because the apprenticeship system of course provides a basis for recruiting the professional skills needed by the defence. So finally, the Committee expresses satisfaction in the vote on the work started to improve the situation of veterans who are suffering from sensitised injuries after participation in international operations. Since the second world war in Germany, Norway has had personnel in international military operations — 120 000 in total for 40 operations. That's part of it. These are men and women who have contributed to increasing stability in conflict zones. Participation in such operations also contributes to improving the global security situation, not least after we have received asymmetric warfare. Some of our soldiers have given the girlfriend they have, their own lives, in active military service out there. I am proud that the Stortinget, at the end of its presidency and on a proposal from the representative of Hagesetter, has decided to recognise and honor the Norwegian men and women who, with their lives, have contributed to international peace and security. A memory plate is now being set up in the staging building to honor our failings in international operations. With this, I recommend the unanimous vote of the Committee.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e4795	There have been many contentious issues in Stortinget today, so it's good to have a little break with a couple of unanimous issues from the Control and Constitutional Committee – before we go back to it. Review of the State Council's protocols is part of the constitutional control responsibilities of the Stortinget to investigate the government's activities. The Rules of Procedure require the Supervisory Committee and the Constitutional Committee to carry out these checks on behalf of the Storting. So this is kind of a half-yearly view, and it's often gone very quickly because there's been a few comments. This time — if you can use that term — it has been a little more exciting, even though it has been as exciting as the debate on agriculture earlier today. This period has had two dissidents in the government – so the period is the last six months of 2010. Both of these were concerned with the Data Storage Directive. The Committee has also reviewed the rules transmitted and has taken out two specific review regulations. Regulation No 9 July 2010 applies to: 1087 on compensation for restrictions on the use of demarcated pastures due to predatory game, and Regulation of 17 December 2010 on coordinated treatment against winter and spring 2011 salmoneus. None of these regulations has been found to make any comments, but they have been part of the regulatory review that the Committee has now committed itself to. We have also found hand inscriptions on the State Council Protocol on two occasions. One correction has been made due to a typographical error, and the other correction was made in relation to a paragraph reference. The Committee has addressed this issue by letter to the Prime Minister. The Prime Minister has responded to the practice of the Committee. The Committee has stated that it is the kgl. the resolution expressing the real will of the Council of State and agreeing with the Prime Minister that a person must exercise a particularly high degree of caution if he makes corrections in agreed kilograms. resolutions without re-examination by the Council of State. There is a new treatment in the Council of State that is the clear rule, even in relatively small changes. In addition, the Committee has received information relating to the appointment of ambassadors in specific missions. The general rule for the appointment of ambassadors, including ambassadors in special assignments, is that they are to be appointed to the Council of State. The first paragraph of Chapter 2 of Section 16 is quite clear that the general rule is that. However, in some special and very short-term missions of a ceremonial nature, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs may appoint such ambassadors on special missions. It is a common assumption that the use of that title is limited to the duration of the mission, and this should be specified in the individual case. The Committee addressed this issue with the Minister for Foreign Affairs, and the Minister for Foreign Affairs then explained the status of these appointments to the Committee. In the second half of 2010, six ambassadors were appointed without treatment in the Council of State. All titles are assigned from a specific date but without an end date. In addition to those appointed in the last six months of 2010, 12 persons were assigned the title of Ambassador in special assignments, one of them being fully retired from 2004. Eight of these twelve were from a certain date, but without the end date. Of those that had been awarded titles with a specified end date, the appointment was for all more than 16 months. The Committee has criticised this practice and has found reason to state that the general rule of the foreign policy, Chapter 2, Section 16, is that the appointment of ambassadors is also to be carried out on specific missions by the Council of State. The Committee has asked the Minister for Foreign Affairs to implement changes that bring these processes within the framework of the foreign policy. It is an important signal that the Minister of Foreign Affairs should take with him. In addition – and finally – this time we have also found reason to point to the need for the Committee to have access to the expanded candidate lists. We've pointed it out several times before, but still there are some departments that don't follow that call. I ask the Council of State who is present in the Chamber to take it back to their colleagues and ensure that one in the future is cleaned up in such a simple situation. Otherwise, the committee's position is unanimous on absolutely all points.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e4823	This may be the reason why no more have asked for the floor on case No. 18th@item: inlistbox [[Fotation, see page 5006]]
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e4839	The rapporteur, Ine M. Eriksen Soreide, has the floor and has 30 minutes of speaking time.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e4842	That was when a very rough speaking time – it is not necessary at all. As the rapporteur, I intended to take advantage of the occasion in connection with the annual matter we have on ‘Regulatory for additions, etc.’. to released compulsory ratings’, to say a few words about the duty of protection and initial service now following the military action that has just been carried out. As Stortinget is well known, § 109 of the Constitution states: «Every citizen of the State is generally equally obliged, for a certain period of time, to protect his country of origin, without regard to birth or wealth.» So that means that we have a duty of protection, and the first-time service is one of several possible ways to implement that duty of protection. There is a broad consensus in Norway on the protection obligation, and there is also a broad consensus that the first-time service may be the main means of implementing it. A few years ago, it was like approaching the entire male coal, i.e. approximately. 30 000, completed the service, whether they needed it or not. Today it is approx. 10000 to be called and 7000 to 8000 to be executed. There is also a new system of women/girls on session, and it is exciting to see what the outcome of it will be in the long term. As I said, there is broad agreement on the protection and first-time service. However, in order for that consensus to remain there and for the foundation of the duty of protection to remain strong, it is important that we continuously update both the initial service and other means of implementing the duty of protection. This means that we have to make sure that this is a military education you get when you do the first service, that it is real, that you have time-real material, that you have what you need to actually also contribute to recruiting to the Defense. As I said, there has just been a military operation. I had the pleasure of being present at two of the events. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e4889	Pirate attacks outside Somalia, in the Gulf of Aden and in the Indian Sea have increased in scope and intensity while the use of violence by pirates has increased. Since 1 January of this year, pirates have killed at least seven seafarers, and as of 14 May 26 ships and 520 seafarers are held hostage. There is no doubt that the situation is serious and unacceptable. In order to prevent piracy, there are established transits, involving the gathering of ships in groups and sailing in corridors where the military vessels are stationed along the route as watch posts. So the ships don't have a permanent escort, but if a ship sends an alarm, the response time is less than 30 minutes, preferably from a helicopter on one of the military vessels. Convoys are restricted to Gulf of Aden. Due to the presence of the military forces, the Gulf of Aden is relatively safe for shipping today. However, the problem is that pirates operate in large parts of the Indian Ocean, where they are not convoys, and where military presence is not effective enough to protect shipping. In practice, pirate waters are today an area larger than Europe, and it is therefore too easy to say that everything is going to solve with an extra frigate. In order to prevent piracy, the industry has taken initiatives, and it is established Best Management Practices, as we know here in the Chamber, can be undertaken as voluntary measures. Lawlessness in Somalia allows pirate networks to operate from bases along the coast. Pirate base on land is located in the regions of Puntland and Galmudug in the north-east. International measures to combat piracy in Somali waters have helped to some extent to dampen the activity. But the networks are adapting to international efforts. The problem has shifted to new sea areas beyond the reach of the international maritime forces. The pirate networks also develop their methods and adapt to countermeasures from commercial shipping. If the mainland pirate networks in Somalia are not challenged, the defense's intelligence believes that we can expect the problem to increase to an extent. ‘A lasting solution to the problem requires stabilisation of the mainland situation’, they write in the recent threat assessment. Norway spends more than 200 million each year. kr for stabilisation in Somalia. It is also important to say that the measures taken by the industry itself, with physical measures such as barge wire or water cannons along the shooting side, have been effective in the sense that to date no successful hijackings have been carried out by ships that have fully followed all preventive measures. But our main priority in Norwegian policy has been to build up justice in Somalia. This is important, not least to ensure that the pirates get their sentence and must pay their punishment. Nine out of ten pirates captured at sea are released again. This is totally unacceptable, and shows the importance of combating piracy through a broad approach. There is agreement in this Chamber that Norway should also contribute militarily to the fight against piracy. We have done this both with the frigate KNM ‘Fridtjof Nansen’, which was put at the disposal of the EU operation Atalanta for half a year in 2009/2010, and with staff officers in the operation. Now we're going to send an Orion maritime surveillance aircraft from October this year – a capacity that's very useful, and that's requested by NATO, where our defense has good competence through long experience in monitoring our own large seas. And we're particularly good at connecting the intelligence collected through aircraft, along with the ships operating on the sea. The majority in this room therefore consider that the intention of the representative proposal is respected, and that a frigate without a helicopter, which is what the opposition will send in addition, is also not as effective and is therefore not current.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e4908	It is now four months since the proposal for increased Norwegian intervention in the Gulf of Aden was promoted. The minority in the Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence therefore wants to strengthen the NATO operation Ocean Shield by putting a frigate at our disposal again this year, and as soon as possible. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e4957	Norwegian and international seafarers experience a daily fear of piracy in the Gulf of Aden. The situation affects international trade and is unacceptable. Around 1 000 Norwegian ships pass through the Gulf of Aden this year. About half of these fly the Norwegian flag. This means that there are at all times a significant number of Norwegian ships with several hundred Norwegian and foreign seafarers on board these waters. In addition, the vessels that sail in the Indian Ocean. About 20 pst. of all trade in the world passing through the Gulf of Aden. Despite about two years of active international efforts, piracy continues to pose a constant threat to maritime traffic. The pirate situation in the Indian and Arab seas has deteriorated in recent months. The figures from the International Maritime Agency for the first quarter of 2011 indicate that the number of piracy attacks in these areas is increasing. Additional 15 hijackings in the period is a fact. At present, 20 ships are being held by some 457 seafarers in the custody of pirates. The area of action of the Pirates has increased sharply, and they are now operating in action groups in large parts of the Indian and Arab seas. This significant expansion of the area has led to a high need for maritime patrol aeroplanes that can monitor larger sea areas. So far, it has proved difficult to obtain sufficient resources for such monitoring. Especially for NATO, this is a challenge. In addition, pirate operations have become more aggressive, and it is now common for ships to be targeted with automatic weapons and grenades, often from several pirate vessels at the same time. So far this year the pirates have killed seven hostages. A number of Norwegian ships have been attacked and attempted hijacking. Pirate activities pose a major threat to the safety of seafarers. Norwegian shipping companies shall be commended for their thorough prevention of the imposition of countermeasures against piracy. This effort has undoubtedly contributed to the fact that Norway has so far been cautious about the hijacking of ships. However, we must be prepared that Norwegian ships or ships with Norwegian crews may also be hijacked. The ransom payments paid for the release of hijacked vessels are increasing. On average, in 2010, $5.4 million per ship was paid, compared with $15,000 in 2005. Such developments underline the need to intensify efforts to better understand the flow of money and the laundering of income from piracy. Military efforts are one important component in the fight against piracy. The Government therefore decided, as it is known on 4 April, to initiate the planning for the deployment of a Norwegian P-3N Orion maritime patrol aircraft for a period of up to three months in the autumn of 2011 as part of the NATO operation Ocean Shield. At the same time, it was decided to send two staff officers to the NATO Shipping Centre for up to eight months. Officers are already in place and will contribute to improving coordination towards international shipping. Both NATO and the EU have expressed the need for vessels with helicopters on board and maritime patrol aeroplanes. Both the EU and NATO are calling for military contributions. This applies in particular to the NATO operation Ocean Shield. Since the launch of this operation, NATO has had a continuous undercoverage on ships and maritime patrol aircraft. Specially the lack of maritime patrol aircraft is perceived as problematically given the extended operating range. The Norwegian patrol flight contribution has thus been considered to be the best response to the military need in the area. The information from the patrol aircraft is reported to the NATO operation and will also be shared with the EU operation in the area. The reason why the patrol flight contribution is three months old is because we don't want the contribution to the Gulf of Aden to go at the expense of our national ability to monitor our northern seas. It is the military needs that have been unequivocally expressed both in NATO and in the EU that have been the governing force for Norway to decide that our contribution this time will be a maritime patrol plane. The EU established its maritime operation, Atalanta, on 8 December 2008. The aim is to escort the World Food Programme supply ship to Somalia and protect vulnerable shipping from piracy. Norway participated with the frigate KNM ‘Fridtjof Nansen’ with a crew of 160 for a period of six months until February 2010. The mission was handled in a very good way, and the operations management in Atalanta considered the Norwegian contribution to be very important. Norway is still represented by 15 permanent staff officers at NATO Maritime Headquarters in Northwood outside London.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-16.ana.ud1149e5177	Let me first say a little about some of the issues in the setting that there has not been any particular attention. The border guard at the Garrison in South Varanger is doing an important job of guarding Norway’s border with Russia – a border that is also the external border of the Schengen countries. Today, the border guard has six so-called border stations, where parts of the building mass are old and inappropriate. It is therefore now set up to replace these six stations with two new, modern stations – one at Svanvik in Pasvik and one near Storskog. The size has previously decided that the defence trade unions in information and communication technologies will gather as much as possible at Jørstadmoen at Lillehammer. This follows the government up, and the increased activity on Jørstadmoen means that there is now a need for more enrichment capacity. Therefore, it is gratifying that there is full support for the defence to build new command cabinets and new leisure fairs at Jørstadmoen Over several years, the Defence Agency has been working on a common integrated management system – the LOS programme. The so-called Logistics Project is the third and last major project in the LOS programme. The implementation of this project exceeds the previously adopted cost framework and significantly. The Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence considered that the project could not be stopped at this time, but it is not easy for us to agree to increase the cost framework for the project, and I ask the defense that they note some critical comments from the Committee. Let me also say quite briefly that the Foreign Affairs and Defence Committee has no comments on the matters put forward by the government in the proposal for information for the Stortinget. It concerns the Eastern Region, the work on energy savings in defence, the purchase of a new research vessel, the status of the national helicopter competence centre and the work on organisational changes at the Air Force Main Workshop at Kjeller. Let me then turn to the big case, namely the acquisition of training planes to the Air Force and the further development of the Joint Strike Missile, JSM. On 6 June 1975, The Stortinget decided by 100 to 19 votes to purchase 72 fighter aircraft of type F-16. The 36 years since then have shown that it was a wise, right, and forward - looking decision. When F-16 arrived in 1980, it was a great technological and operational leap for the Defense. But we've now had F-16 for 31 years, and they're still going to be used for 10 new years. The planes are getting old. We have to make sure to have new aircraft in place that can replace F-16 when the day comes when they will be put on the ground. Norway is a long-distance country with large areas both at sea and at sea — and with similar large airspace. That's why an operational fighter weapon is extra important in a country like ours. New fighter aircraft are essential for the defence to be able to maintain our security and sovereignty satisfactorily in the future. There seems to be a lot of agreement about that. But then we have to make sure to make the right decisions, so that we have new planes that can take over the day F-16 is no longer operational. Acquiring new fighter aircraft is a comprehensive and time-consuming process. In 2009, Stortinget decided that F-35 would be Norway’s future fighter aircraft. F-35 was the best plane, the plane that could best meet the requirements of Norway. In addition, it was cheaper than the ‘competitor’ to use such a concept. Norway participates in the multinational F-35 programme together with eight other countries. The development programme has been somewhat delayed. This also affects Norway's deliveries, and the first aircraft are now scheduled to be delivered in 2018. Given that the F-16 aircraft will then be near 40 years old, this city will face challenges. It is limited how long you can upgrade and maintain such old airframes. Therefore, the government has proposed to the Stortinget that it purchase four F-35 training aircraft, which can be delivered in 2016 – two years before the main aircraft supply itself comes. In this way, we can start with training of pilots, technicians and other support staff before the main delivery starts in 2018. If we want these training flights in 2016, the order must be notified in August of this year and must be confirmed in December. If we do not report the order in August, we have to wait a year, and we only get training aircraft delivered in 2017. Does a year from or to really play such a big role? To that question, I will answer an unconditional yes – considering the age of the F-16 planes. With four training aeroplanes in 2016 and the main delivery from 2018, we will receive a phase-in phase-in of F-35 while gradually phase-out of F-16. Let me then comment on some of the issues that have been a bit of noise around – not in the committee or in the committee's setting, but in the media there has been noise, partly caused by some of the same representatives in the committee. In the committee setting they are in favour of the flight purchase, in the media they are against. It's been claimed that someone doesn't know what the price of the planes is. But for the four training flights we have today for treatment, we know the price. It's $800 million. kr per piece, SEK 3,2 billion. SEK in total, and with the provision for uncertainty – as is to be done for such projects – the cost framework is SEK 4,8 billion. kr. There's no uncertainty about it. For the other aircraft to be delivered from 2018, the price is not yet completely clear, sail if we have good calculations on what it will be on. The reason why the price is not ready is that the aircraft is not fully developed. In recent public vocabulary, there have been many figures about what the purchase of combat aircraft will cost – a rather confusing debate that many have not wanted to hear the correct figures. Let me therefore try to explain the cost development of the fighter aircraft programme: In St. prp. No 36 for 2008-2009, the acquisition costs of the new F-35 aeroplanes were estimated to be approximately 42 billion. kr, present value 2008-krona. With technical adjustment for price growth, changes in exchange rates and without discount, this equals approximately 60 billion. 2011 krones, calculated in exactly the same way as one calculates other similar acquisitions. The acquisition costs are now estimated at 61 billion. So in 2011, the purchase price has increased by 1 billion. kr since the 2008 calculations. And then where does the amount of 72 billion come from? kr from? Well, that's the amount of $61 billion. in addition to the provisions for uncertainty, as required by the legislation, in the case of worst-case outcomes. But it's 61 billion. kr, which is the number today, and the increase is 1 billion. NOK – not 20, not 40, not 50, as someone has claimed. Next year, Stortinget will deal with the main acquisition of new fighter aircraft. We would also like to have an up-to-date decision base, including what goes on price. If one doesn't want a rematch on the flight selection itself, what's the problem with ordering the four training planes now, all the time next year we're going to do a thorough treatment of the main acquisition of new fighter planes? Uncertainty about the price may not be – the price of these four planes we know. Why can't we wait with the decision on training airplanes until next year? Well, we can do that, but then we get the planes in 2017 and not in 2016. Some have said that we should postpone the decision until the fall. It would have been very special since the flight purchase had to be reported in August. If we had postponed the matter until the fall, we might as well have postponed it to the next year. I am pleased that the Stortinget today makes the decision to go to the purchase of four F-35 training planes. Then we'll get back to the rest of the fighter aircraft program – including the price and cost question – when we're going to deal with the main delivery next year. Let me end by mentioning the further development of the Joint Strike Missile – JSM – that we are also going to make today's decision. JSM is an essential part of Norway’s aircraft acquisition. There is full agreement that we should contribute to the development of stage 2 of the JSM programme. It's very joyful. If we succeed in that, we'll get a fighter weapon with a world-class missile, while at the same time it could be an industrial venture for Norwegian companies, maybe without equal. Other speakers from the Workers' Party will get closer to it. I recommend with this committee position at the warmest and, at the same time, would also encourage those who are behind it to vote against the proposal from the Left, which has been promoted today.

The entire proposition is seven sides long, and the review of the training planes only occupies two sides. The cost estimate for both the purchase of training aircraft and the main acquisition in 2018 is presented in just under 200 words – or on a quarter of a page. The figures are incomparable, and the information appears to be completely cryptic and inaccessible, sea for sharp-sheared pine knuckles. For a purchase that binds us even more strongly to the largest investment ever in mainland Norway, two sides appear to be very deficient. And that's a completely incomplete decision-making basis. It was against this background that the right of the committee took an initiative to organise an open hearing. It was held on June 6. In addition to the hearing, the Committee considered it necessary to submit further questions, both before and after consultation. All questions and answers as well as the full minutes of the hearing are attached to the submission, and I refer you to the answers given orally in the hearing and in writing in response to questions submitted. The questions were not primarily about details but about completely basic information about the acquisition of fighter aircraft. The questions raised include: price, price development since 2008, delivery time, status of the JSF project in the United States and in other partner countries. This is information that would obviously have been available already in the proposition, and it should actually not be necessary for the Storting to ask for that kind of information. Here Stortinget has done the job that the government should have done before submitting the case to Stortinget. In this context, I refer in particular to answers to questions 2 to 4 in Annex 3 and answers to questions 1 to 8 in Annex 6. From the Right, we would stress that what appears in the answers to be a technical adjustment for price growth is in reality more than a significant increase in the cost framework for acquisition. Regardless of whether the price change is due to currency changes, whether it is due to the project's shift further in time, or whether it is due to other factors, it is equally full of real price increases and must be explicitly made by the Ministry as it is by the Stortinget. Such a procedure by the Department in such an important case is not, in the Court's view, satisfactory. We expect the ministry to take up this criticism and provide basic and essential information in a detailed way in future propositions, especially when the proposition on the main acquisition of fighter aircraft is transmitted to the Stortinget in a short year's time. There are still outstanding questions, and there is little doubt that the Right would prefer to wait with the decision of the overall picture available. Nevertheless, a point has to be found on which to base the presentation of the Department and the Council of State.
