I quote part of the answer to question 5, from the right, in attachment 3: ‘In total, the Ministry of Defence’s assessment is that the cost picture, including the total lifetime costs, has not changed significantly and is within the uncertainty estimate from 2008, both in terms of purchase costs and operating costs.’;» Of course, the large nominal increase in acquisition costs and the considerable concern about overruns in the programme in the United States have also raised the Committee's concerns. When the Department and the Council of State assure the Storting that everything goes according to plan, we assume that that's right. This is important for the Right, because a high cost of lifetime costs from a 30-year perspective could, of course, have consequences for the operation of other defence sectors. We have to have fighter planes, but we also have to have money to operate the other defence structure. Secondly — and this is the most important point for the Right — the Ministry informs that the suspension of the decision on the acquisition of training aeroplanes could go beyond preparedness and sovereignty enforcement for a transitional period between F-16 and F-35. In other words, not to report an order for training aeroplanes in August will delay delivery and provide reduced preparedness. What was an assessment on our part was to postpone this until spring 2012, when the Stortinge gets the whole of the treatment of the main acquisition, base selection and all the other elements of the long-term plan for treatment. All relevant information will then be available, and then the acquisition can also be seen better in the light of the long-term plan and up-to-date lifetime costs. Ideally, then, we would have dealt with this proposition. This is the largest investment ever made by mainland Norway, and also a partial acquisition, which the proposition implies, deserves such thorough treatment. Nevertheless, according to the answers we have been given, it could have delayed the phase-in of F-35 for so long that the preparedness would be weakened. A period of such a lack of preparedness is not acceptable to the Right, and in this field we have no choice but to base the department's assessment. We believe it would be irresponsible to take a chance on such a lack of preparedness, sail if it were only a short period of time. This information I have now mentioned has been a condition for the right-hand side to now choose to support the proposition – rather than to imagine that it would be postponed to the next session. After several rounds of questions, answers and hearings, we must assume that the information provided to the Stortinget is correct and complete, and that the Council of State is responsible to the Stortingen for the information provided. The acquisition of a total of 56 F-35 fighter aircraft will mean that the Norwegian Air Force will have a state-of-the-art high-tech capacity at the height of the very best in the world for many years to come. It is our responsibility as politicians on the Stortinget and in the Department that the transition between F-16 and F-35 will go as painless as possible and without significant consequences for preparedness and sovereignty enforcement. Right will do what is necessary to get this done, and we also expect the Storing and the Government to contribute.

It was not just that the government claimed that the F-35 was the best and cheapest. The professional assessment of the two flight options included in the final phase did not allow for doubt. Only the F-35 could handle the tasks that the new fighter aircraft was supposed to do. The acquisition of the new fighter aircraft will be the largest investment project over time in mainland Norway. Good governance and control of this investment project is therefore also economically important. It is therefore worrying that the development of Lockheed Martin's fighter aircraft project F-35 has been delayed for several years and more expensive than planned. Significant cost increases and challenges have been identified through studies conducted by the US authorities. The Christian People's Party believes that these critical assessments are not reasonably reflected in the present investment proposition. That view is shared by the majority of the Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence Policy. In the present investment proposition, the government is asking for the consent of the Storting to purchase the first four fighter aircraft of type F-35 – within a cost framework of 4.8 billion. kr. The price tag on the acquisition of all the scheduled 56 aircraft has risen to 61 billion. kr – maybe 72 brd. kr if the uncertainty margin has to be used in full. In addition, annual base and operating costs are incurred over several decades. In the prototype, the number of the four fighter aeroplanes concerned for training purposes is less than two pages. It won't last. The story therefore seemed to have to have a separate hearing and ask many dozens of written questions about the fighter aircraft case. It is our opinion that, despite this, we are left with several unanswered questions. An updated estimate of the lifetime costs of the F-35 project has not been submitted to the Committee. The implications of the rest of the Air Force structure cannot be said by the Council of State at this stage. The best thing would have been if the decision could have been made next spring. Then there would be both an up-to-date estimate of the lifetime costs of the fighter aircraft project and the next long-term defence plan. Such a postponement of the decision time has been rejected by the Council of State, claiming that it would have two serious consequences. Firstly, Norway cannot be supplied with training aircraft until 2017, if the Stortinget does not make a decision now, and the order is made in the autumn. The same deadline applies to other partner countries. Secondly, if we do not get training flights until 2017, the training of pilots for the new planes is delayed. As a result, the operational capability of the fighting aircraft in a transitional phase may fall below a certain level and Norway’s ability to assert its sovereignty will be significantly impaired. The last thing we think is very serious. The Christian People's Party must therefore take note of the Council's response to this point. From this we will support a decision to acquire the four training planes, although the decision base is far from satisfactory and should have been wider on the two points I have mentioned. In addition, the Committee has had to do a lot of the work that the Ministry had not done in the proposal in order to obtain the information. That's exactly the point that several speakers from the opposition have made. To the SV's parliamentary leader, I have to say that if SV attacks the opposition and the Christian People's Party to be critical of the decision-making basis, but supports the fighter flight purchase, there is only one thing to say: it must be an internal confusion in the SV because the party is now under government responsibility to propose the first step in the greatest Norwegian purchase of American fighter aircraft with associated bombs and rockets. There is also reason to remember that it is the red-green government, including SV, that has claimed that it can weaken Norway's preparedness and our ability to assert Norwegian sovereignty, unless the Storting here and now decides to buy the first four US fighter aircraft of type F-35. When SV is responsible for proposing a combat aircraft investment that surpasses all previous weapons investments, perhaps the SV parliamentary leader should go a little slower in the doors – rather than go after others.
